Logarithmic geometry beyond fs
Piotr Achinger, Katharina Hübner, Marcin Lara, Jakob Stix

Abstract




Abstract
— We develop the foundations of logarithmic structures beyond the standard finiteness conditions. The motivation is the study of semistable models over general valuation rings. The key new notion is that of a morphism of finite presentation up to saturation (sfp), which is one that is qcqs and which is locally isomorphic to the saturated base change of a finitely presented morphism between fs log schemes. As in the case of schemes, sfp maps can (locally on the base) be approximated by maps between fs log schemes of finite type over
\mathds{Z}
. Based on sfp maps, we define smooth, étale, and Kummer étale maps. Importantly, the maps of schemes underlying such maps are no longer of finite type in general, though surprisingly they are if the base is the spectrum of a valuation ring with algebraically closed field of fractions. These foundations allow us to extend beyond the fs case the theory of the Kummer étale site and of the Kummer étale fundamental group.

1.
Introduction

Logarithmic geometry, as envisioned by Fontaine and Illusie and developed by
K. Kato (see
[
Kat89
,
ACG+13
,
Ogu18
]
), is a framework allowing one to deal more easily with compactifications and degenerations in algebraic geometry. For example, a semistable scheme over a discrete valuation ring, equipped with the natural logarithmic structure, becomes smooth in the sense of logarithmic geometry.

However, while the theory of schemes has been famously developed in complete generality, allowing spectra of arbitrary commutative rings as local models, logarithmic geometry has been mostly limited to log structures which locally admit a chart by a finitely generated (or
fs
, for finitely generated and saturated) monoid. Indeed, the notion of a log structure seems to be too general to produce meaningful universal results — e.g., sheaves of differentials are not always quasi-coherent. Even if one restricts their attention to log structures which locally admit a chart (by a possibly not finitely generated monoid), called quasi-coherent, one quickly runs into foundational issues, related e.g. to the existence of charts for morphisms (see Remarks
3.1.3
and
3.1.4
).

There are situations of geometric interest where the natural log structures do not satisfy the customary finiteness conditions. For example, the standard log structure on the spectrum of a valuation ring
K^{+}
with fraction field
K
, divided modulo units, is the non-negative part
\Gamma_{K}^{+}=(K^{+}\cap K^{\times})/(K^{+})^{\times}
of its value group
\Gamma_{K}=K^{\times}/(K^{+})^{\times}
. This monoid will not be finitely generated unless
K^{+}
is a field or a discrete valuation ring. Therefore, in order to deal with semistable models over more general valuation rings such as
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathds{C}}_{p}}
, one has to work with monoids which, while not being finitely generated, are (close to being) finitely generated over the “base” monoid
\Gamma^{+}_{K}
.

The goal of this paper is to develop the necessary foundations of logarithmic structures beyond the category of fs log schemes, with a particular focus on the Kummer étale topology and on log schemes over (not necessarily discrete) valuation rings. In the context of semistable reduction and prismatic cohomology, similar though less general approaches have been suggested and/or developed by Koshikawa
[
Kos22
]
, Adiprasito–Liu–Pak–Temkin
[
ALPT19
]
, Zavyalov
[
Zav24
]
, and Diao–Yao
[
DY24
]
. Our work grew out of a need for a more general theory of the Kummer étale fundamental group, which arose in the proof of finite generation of the tame fundamental group of a rigid-analytic space in
[
AHLS24
]
.

The new basic notion, introduced in
Definition

2.2.6
, is that of an sfp (finitely presented up to saturation) morphism of saturated
1
1

1

We will mostly deal with saturated monoids, due to our intended applications. One can develop a similar story for integral monoids without putting in more effort.
monoids.

Definition A

(
Definition

2.2.6
)
.

A morphism of saturated monoids
P\to Q
is
sfp
(finitely presented up to saturation) if it admits a factorisation

P\longrightarrow Q^{\prime}\longrightarrow Q

where
P\to Q^{\prime}
is a morphism of finite presentation (where
Q^{\prime}
is not necessarily saturated or integral) and where
Q^{\prime}\to Q
induces an isomorphism
(Q^{\prime})^{\rm sat}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}Q
.

Equivalently,
P\to Q
is sfp if
Q=(P\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0})^{\rm sat}
for a homomorphism of fs monoids
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
and a map
P_{0}\to P
. Sfp morphisms
P\to Q
are precisely the compact objects of the category of saturated monoids over
P
. Based on this definition, we define smooth, étale, and Kummer étale homomorphisms between saturated monoids.

Definition B

(
Definition

2.4.4
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a set of primes and let
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
be a morphism of saturated monoids.

(1)

We say that
\vartheta
is
smooth
if it is sfp, and if the kernel and the torsion part of the cokernel of
\vartheta^{\rm gp}\colon P^{\rm gp}\to Q^{\rm gp}
are of order prime to all
p\in\Sigma
.

(2)

The map
\vartheta
is
étale
if it is smooth and if moreover the cokernel of
\vartheta^{\rm gp}
is torsion.

(3)

The map
\vartheta
is
Kummer étale
if it is injective, étale, and exact.

The theory of sfp morphisms of monoids becomes particularly nice over a base valuative monoid, and even better over a divisible valuative monoid. Recall that an integral monoid
V
is valuative if
V^{\rm gp}=V\cup(-V)
. It is divisible if
V^{\rm gp}
is a divisible group. Notably, the monoid
K^{+}\cap K^{\times}
giving the log structure on the spectrum of a valuation ring
K^{+}
is valuative, and is divisible if
K
is algebraically closed. Using the foundational results of F. Kato
[
Kat22
]
and T. Tsuji
[
Tsu19
]
, we show in
Section

2.5
that if
V\to Q
is an sfp morphism, with
V
valuative, then
V\to Q
is integral and after a Kummer étale extension
V\to W
the saturated base change
W\to(Q\oplus_{V}W)^{\rm sat}
is finitely presented and saturated, see
Corollary

2.5.17
(in analogy with rigid-analytic geometry, we call this result the Reduced Fibre Theorem). In particular, if
V
is also divisible, then every sfp morphism
V\to Q
is finitely presented and saturated (an analogue of the Grauert–Remmert finiteness theorem in rigid-analytic geometry). Due to these useful features, and motivated by the geometric applications, we introduce the following notion: a monoid is of type
(\mathrm{V})
(resp. of
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
) if it is sfp over a valuative (resp. divisible valuative) monoid.

In
Section

3
we turn our attention to log schemes. The log schemes we consider are saturated in the sense that they admit local charts by saturated monoids (see
Remark

3.1.1
).

Definition C

(
Definition

3.3.1
and
Remarks

3.3.2
)
.

A morphism of saturated log schemes
Y\to X
is
locally sfp
if étale locally on source and target it admits a chart by an sfp morphism of monoids
P\to Q
such that the induced strict map

Y\longrightarrow X\times_{\Spec({\mathds{Z}}[P])}\Spec({\mathds{Z}}[Q])

is of finite presentation (as a morphism of schemes). We say that
Y\to X
is
sfp
if it is locally sfp and qcqs.

If
X
is an fs log scheme, a (locally) sfp map
Y\to X
is just a morphism of fs log schemes whose underlying map of schemes is (locally) of finite presentation. Crucially, we show in
Corollary

3.3.4
that (locally) sfp morphisms enjoy a “chart lifting property”, which allows us to show e.g. that they are stable under composition and saturated base change. One of our main results concerning sfp morphisms of log schemes is that a qcqs map
Y\to X
is sfp if and only if étale locally
on
X
it is the saturated base change of a map of fs log schemes of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
. More generally, we obtain the following approximation result in the style of
[
EGAIV3
, Théorème 8.5.2]
.

Theorem D

(
Theorem

3.4.5
)
.

Let
\{X_{i}\}
be an affine charted inverse system (
Definition

3.4.4
) of qcqs saturated log schemes and let
X=\varprojlim X_{i}
be its inverse limit. Denote by
\mathbf{Sfp}_{X}
(resp.
\mathbf{Sfp}_{X_{i}}
) the category of sfp morphisms with target
X
(resp.
X_{i}
). Then, saturated base change induces an equivalence of categories

\varinjlim_{i\in I}\mathbf{Sfp}_{X_{i}}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}\mathbf{Sfp}_{X}.

This theorem allows us to reduce many questions regarding saturated log schemes to the case of fs log structures studied in the literature. One might hope that it could be supplemented with an “absolute approximation” result in the style of Thomason–Trobaugh
[
TT90
, Theorem C.9]
, to the effect that every qcqs saturated log scheme can be expressed as the inverse limit
X=\varprojlim X_{i}
of an affine charted inverse system where
X_{i}
are fs and of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
. However, we show in
Example

3.4.8
that this hope is too optimistic.

Again, the notion of an sfp morphism is the base for the definitions of smooth, étale, and Kummer étale morphisms.

Definition E

(
Definition

3.5.1
)
.

A morphism of saturated log schemes
Y\to X
is
smooth
(resp.
étale
, resp.
Kummer étale
) if étale locally on source and target it admits a chart by a smooth (resp. étale, resp. Kummer étale) morphism of monoids
P\to Q
(with
\Sigma
the set of primes non-invertible on
X
) such that the induced strict map

Y\longrightarrow X\times_{\Spec({\mathds{Z}}[P])}\Spec({\mathds{Z}}[Q])

is smooth (resp. étale, resp. étale). In particular, such a morphism is locally sfp.

It is important to note that the morphisms of schemes underlying smooth maps might not be of finite type. For example, we prove in
Section

4.4
that if
(K,K^{+})
is a henselian valued field and
L/K
is a tamely ramified finite separable extension, then with the standard log structures the morphism
\Spec(L^{+})\to\Spec(K^{+})
is Kummer étale. However, we show in
Lemma

2.5.4
that it very rarely happens that
L^{+}
is finite over
K^{+}
if
K^{+}
is not a discrete valuation ring (see
Section

4.4
for explicit examples).

With these foundations in place, we study the Kummer étale topology
X_{\text{\rm k\'{e}t}}
and the Kummer étale fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
in
Section

4
. The theory largely parallels the fs case.
Note, however, that
finite
Kummer étale covers are Kummer étale maps
Y\to X
whose underlying map of schemes is integral (but not necessarily finite). It therefore takes some effort to descend such maps to finite Kummer étale morphisms between fs log schemes (see
Proposition

4.2.6
). The following theorem summarizes the results.

Theorem F
.

Let
X
be a saturated log scheme.

(1)

(Subcanonicality,
Proposition

4.1.6
) The site
X_{\text{\rm k\'{e}t}}
is subcanonical (representable presheaves are sheaves).

(2)

(Topological invariance,
Proposition

4.1.7
) If
X_{0}\to X
is a strict universal homeomorphism, then
X_{\text{\rm k\'{e}t}}\simeq X_{0,\text{\rm k\'{e}t}}
.

(3)

(Coherence,
Proposition

prop:limit-Ket-topos
1
) If
X
is qcqs, then
\mathbf{Sh}(X_{\text{\rm k\'{e}t}})
is a coherent topos.

(4)

(Approximation,

Proposition

prop:limit-Ket-topos
2
) If
X=\varprojlim X_{i}
is the inverse limit of an affine charted inverse system of qcqs saturated log schemes, then
\mathbf{Sh}(X_{\text{\rm k\'{e}t}})\simeq\varprojlim\mathbf{Sh}(X_{i,\text{\rm k\'{e}t}})
.

(5)

(Descent,
Proposition

4.3.6
)
The category
\mathbf{F\acute{E}t}_{X}
of finite Kummer étale maps
Y\to X
is equivalent to the category
\mathbf{lcc}(X_{\text{\rm k\'{e}t}})
of locally constant Kummer étale sheaves of finite sets on
X
.

As a rather formal consequence of
5
, if
X
is connected and
\overline{x}\to X
is a log geometric point, then
\mathbf{F\acute{E}t}_{X}
is a Galois category on which
\overline{x}
induces a fibre functor. We denote by
\pi_{1}(X,\overline{x})
the corresponding fundamental group and call it the
Kummer étale fundamental group
of
X
. We exemplify it in the case of spectra of valuation rings with their standard log structure by showing that the Kummer étale fundamental group agrees with the tame Galois group of the fraction field, which is a non-noetherian variant of Abhyankar’s lemma.

Theorem G

(Abhyankar’s Lemma,
Corollary

4.4.9
)
.

Let
K^{+}
be a henselian valuation ring with fraction field
K
. We let
S_{K}=\Spec(K^{+}\cap K^{\times}\to K^{+})
be the scheme
\Spec(K^{+})
endowed with the log structure charted by the valuative monoid
K^{+}\cap K^{\times}
. Then, there exists a canonical isomorphism of profinite groups

\Gal^{\rm t}(K^{\rm sep}/K)\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}\pi_{1}(S_{K},\star)

between the the tame quotient
\Gal^{\rm t}(K^{\rm sep}/K)
of the absolute Galois group
\Gal(K^{\rm sep}/K)
and the Kummer étale fundamental group of
S_{K}
.

The final
Section

5
is devoted to smooth log schemes over the spectrum of a valuation ring
K^{+}
endowed with the standard log structure. We first introduce, in analogy with non-archimedean geometry, the following notion: a saturated log scheme is of type
(\mathrm{V})
(resp.
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
) if it locally admits a chart by a monoid of
(\mathrm{V})
(resp.
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
). Thus, if
X
a log scheme which is locally sfp over
\Spec(K^{+})
, then
X
is of type
(\mathrm{V})
, and of type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
if the fraction field
K
of
K^{+}
is algebraically closed. The key result, proved using Zavyalov’s approximation lemma
[
Zav24
, Lemma A.2]
, is the following.

Theorem H

(
Proposition

5.3.1
)
.

Let
K^{+}
be a microbial valuation ring with fraction field
K
. Suppose that
K^{+}
is discretely valued or of residue characteristic zero or that
K
is algebraically closed. Endow
S=\Spec(K^{+})
with the log structure charted by the valuative monoid
K^{+}\cap K^{\times}
. Let
X\to S
be a smooth and vertical morphism of saturated log schemes. Then the log structure on
X
agrees with the one induced by the open subset
X\times_{S}\Spec(K)
.

This above theorem (or rather its variant for formal schemes) will play an important role in our subsequent paper
[
AHLS24
]
, allowing us to relate the Kummer étale fundamental group of the special fibre to the (suitably defined) tame fundamental group of the rigid-analytic generic fibre. We expect the theory developed here to be useful in other contexts as well.

1.1.
Notation and conventions

•

We denote the category of finite sets by
\mathbf{sets}
.

•

The symbols
\varinjlim
and
\varprojlim
denote filtered colimits and cofiltered limits, respectively. We use the symbols
\lim
and
\operatorname{colim}
to denote more general limits and colimits.

•

For an object
X
of a category
{\mathcal{C}}
, we denote by
{\mathcal{C}}_{/X}
the category of objects over
X
(morphisms
Y\to X
) and by
{\mathcal{C}}_{X/}
the category of objects under
X
(morphisms
X\to Z
).

•

{\mathds{N}}=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
considered as a monoid with
+
.

•

We denote log schemes by single symbols e.g.
X
(not
(X,{\mathcal{M}}_{X})
) and the underlying scheme is denoted by
\underline{X}
. For log schemes and maps between them, we use adjectives like regular, smooth, étale to mean the respective notions in log geometry (log regular, log smooth, log étale). In particular, if
X
is a saturated log scheme, we denote by
\mathbf{F\acute{E}t}_{X}
the category of finite Kummer étale maps to
X
, see
Definition

4.2.1
.

•

The underlying topological space of a (log) scheme
X
is denoted by
|X|
.

•

The monoid structure on
{\mathcal{M}}_{X}
and on abstract monoids is written additively.

•

Fibre products of integral (resp. saturated) log schemes within the category of integral (resp. saturated) log schemes will be denoted by

X\times_{S}^{{\rm int}}Y\qquad\text{(resp.\ }X\times_{S}^{{\rm sat}}Y\text{ )}.

•

Unless otherwise specified, ‘locally’ or ‘étale locally’ means ‘strict étale locally,’ or equivalently ‘étale locally on the underlying scheme.’

•

The valuation ring of a valued field
K
is denoted
K^{+}
. Its value group is denoted by
\Gamma_{K}
, and its set of non-negative elements by
\Gamma^{+}_{K}
. The monoid structure on
\Gamma^{+}_{K}
is typically written additively, for compatibility with the convention in log geometry.

2.
Monoids beyond fs

In this section, we develop the theory of commutative monoids needed for the extension of logarithmic geometry beyond the setting of fs log schemes in
Section

3
. The natural relative version of an fs monoid is that of a map of saturated monoids
P\to Q
which is finitely presented up to saturation (sfp for short), meaning that
Q
is the saturation of a finitely presented monoid over
P
(
Definition

2.2.6
). The reason for not working simply with maps of finite presentation is that they are not preserved under pushouts in the category of saturated monoids, while of course we want natural classes of morphisms of saturated log schemes (log smooth, Kummer étale etc.) to be preserved under pullback. The map
P\to Q
being sfp means precisely that
Q
is a compact object in the category of saturated monoids over
P
, or that it arises via saturated pushout from a map of fs monoids
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
(
Proposition

2.2.10
). This allows one to reduce questions about sfp maps to the case of fs monoids, similarly to how one reduces questions about finitely presented maps of qcqs schemes to the case of schemes of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
.

The geometric situation in need of such an extension is that of a semistable scheme over a general valuation ring
K^{+}
. In this case, the ‘base’ monoid is the non-negative part
\Gamma_{K}^{+}
of the value group
\Gamma_{K}
of
K
, which will not be finitely generated unless
K
is trivially or discretely valued. However, the monoid
\Gamma_{K}^{+}
is
valuative
: for every element
x\in\Gamma_{K}=(\Gamma_{K}^{+})^{\rm gp}
, either
x\in\Gamma_{K}^{+}
or
-x\in\Gamma_{K}^{+}
. As we shall see, the theory of monoids which are sfp over a valuative monoid has numerous favourable properties. We call them monoids of type
(\mathrm{V})
, and in the case when the valuative monoid is divisible, of type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
, see
Section

2.5
.

Remark 2.0.1

(Analogies with non-archimedean geometry)
.

Our approach is guided by analogy with non-archimedean geometry, and our terminology reflects this. A formal scheme of type
(\mathrm{V})
is one locally of the form
\operatorname{Spf}({\mathcal{A}})
for an algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
topologically of finite presentation (tfp) over a complete microbial valuation ring
K^{+}

[
Bos14
, §7.3]
. A theorem of Nagata
[
Nag66
, Theorem 3]
, generalized by Raynaud and Gruson
[
RG71
, Corollaire I 3.4.7]
, implies that a topologically finitely generated (tft)
K^{+}
-algebra which is torsion-free is automatically finitely presented. Suppose
K^{+}
is of rank one, let
A
be a geometrically reduced affinoid
K
-algebra, and let
A^{\circ}
denote its subring of powerbounded elements. By the Grauert–Remmert finiteness theorem
[
BGR84
, Corollary 6.4.1/5]
, if
K
is either discretely valued or stable
[
BGR84
, §3.6]
and its value group
\Gamma
is divisible (e.g.
K=\overline{K}
), then
A^{\circ}
is tfp over
K^{\circ}=K^{+}
. In general, this may fail even if
A
is a finite separable extension of
K

[
BGR84
, 3.6.1, Example]
. However, one can find a tfp
K^{\circ}
-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
with
A={\mathcal{A}}_{K}
, and then
A^{\circ}
equals the integral closure of
{\mathcal{A}}
in
A

[
Bos14
, Theorem 3.1/17]
. Thus
A^{\circ}
is “finitely presented up to integral closure.” Moreover, by the Reduced Fibre Theorem
[
BLR95
, Theorem 1.3]
, after passing to a finite extension
L
of
K
we achieve that
A^{\circ}
is tfp.

Similarly, let
V
be a valuative monoid, let
P^{\prime}
be a finitely presented monoid over
V
, and let
P
be its saturation. While
V\to P
is sfp by definition, it may happen that
P
is no longer finitely presented (or even finitely generated) over
V
, see
Example

2.5.3
. However, using results of F. Kato and T. Tsuji we show that this is true if
V
is valuative and divisible (
Corollary

2.5.18
). Moreover, one can always make
P
finitely presented by passing to a “finite” extension of
V
(
Corollary

2.5.17
). One also has a version of the finite presentation result of Nagata (
Corollary

2.5.9
), as well as a version of “Néron desingularisation” (
Proposition

2.5.11
).

2.1.
Preliminaries on monoids

All monoids are commutative and the operations are typically written additively. We recall some basic notions
[
Ogu18
, Chapter I]
.

Integral and saturated monoids

For a monoid
P
, we denote by
P\to P^{\rm gp}
its initial homomorphism into a group, and call
P

integral
if this map is injective. We call
P

fine
if it is integral and finitely generated. A monoid
P
is
saturated
if it is integral and for every
p\in P^{\rm gp}
and
n\geq 1
such that
np\in P
, we have
p\in P
. A monoid
P
is
fs
(fine and saturated) if it is finitely generated and saturated. The subgroup of invertible elements of
P
is denoted by
P^{\times}
, and we say that
P
is
sharp
if
P^{\times}=0
.

For an integral monoid
M
and a subset
S
, the
saturation of
S
in
M
consists of all
p\in M
for which there exists an
n\geq 1
such that
np
belongs to the submonoid of
M
generated by
S
. The inclusion of integral (resp. saturated) monoids into all monoids admits a left adjoint, the
integralisation

P\mapsto P^{\rm int}=\im(P\to P^{\rm gp})
(resp. the
saturation

P\mapsto P^{\rm sat}
defined to be the saturation of
P^{\rm int}
as a subset of
P^{\rm gp}
).

Lemma 2.1.1
.

Let
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
be an injective map of integral monoids. Then
\vartheta^{\rm gp}\colon P^{\rm gp}\to Q^{\rm gp}
is injective.

Proof.

The groupification
M^{\rm gp}
of a monoid
M
can be constructed explicitly as
M\times M/\sim
, where

(x,y)\sim(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\iff\exists{z\in M}:\quad x+y^{\prime}+z=x^{\prime}+y+z.

If
M
is integral, then the cancellation law holds for
M
and so
z
can be omitted.

If
\vartheta^{\rm gp}([(x,y)])=0
in
Q^{\rm gp}
, then
(\vartheta(x),\vartheta(y))\sim(0,0)
holds. In other words
\vartheta(x)
equals
\vartheta(y)
, since
Q
is integral. As
\vartheta
is injective,
x
equals
y
, and thus

[(x,y)]=0
and
\vartheta^{\rm gp}
is injective.
∎

Limits and colimits

The categories of monoids, integral monoids, and saturated monoids admit all limits and colimits, see
[
Ogu18
, Chapter I §1.1]
. The inclusion functors preserve limits (being right adjoints) and importantly also filtered colimits
[
Ogu18
, Proposition I 1.3.6]
. To avoid confusion, we will denote by
P\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0}
the pushout of
P\leftarrow P_{0}\to Q_{0}
in the category of monoids. In analogy with the theory of commutative rings, such a pushout will often be referred to as the
base change
of
Q_{0}
to
P
. If
P
,
P_{0}
, and
Q_{0}
are saturated, the corresponding pushout
Q
in the category of saturated monoids admits the following description (compare
[
DY24
, §2.4]
)

Q=(P\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0})^{\rm sat}\quad\subseteq\quad Q^{\rm gp}=P^{\rm gp}\oplus_{P_{0}^{\rm gp}}Q_{0}^{\rm gp}

is the saturation of the union of the images of
P
and
Q_{0}
in
Q^{\rm gp}
. We will also refer to it as the
saturated base change
of
Q_{0}
to
P
.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

Let
P\to Q
be the pushout (resp. integral or saturated pushout) of a morphism of monoids (resp. integral or saturated monoids)
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
along a map
P_{0}\to P
. Then the natural map

Q_{0}^{\rm gp}/P_{0}^{\rm gp}\longrightarrow Q^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}

is an isomorphism. The natural map

\ker(P_{0}^{\rm gp}\to Q_{0}^{\rm gp})\longrightarrow\ker(P^{\rm gp}\to Q^{\rm gp})

is surjective, and it is even an isomorphism if
P_{0}\to P
is an injective map of integral monoids.

Proof.

Since groupification is left adjoint, it commutes with pushouts. So the lemma actually contains a claim about the pushout in the category of abelian groups. It follows by the snake lemma applied to the diagram (and
Lemma

2.1.1
for the last assertion)

Short exact sequences of monoids

By a
short exact sequence
of monoids we mean a sequence of integral monoids

0\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow M\overset{\varphi}{\longrightarrow}N\longrightarrow 0,

where
G
is a subgroup of
M
and
\varphi\colon M\to N
identifies
N
with the set of
G
-orbits. This is equivalent to saying that the sequence of associated groups is exact and the map
\varphi\colon M\to N
is
exact
(recalled in
Definition

defi:exactintsatvert monoids
1
below). We shall also write
N=M/G
in this situation. In the special case
G=M^{\times}
being the largest subgroup of
M
, we denote
M/G=M/M^{\times}
by
\overline{M}
.

An often useful fact about short exact sequences of monoids is that providing a section of the map
M\to N
is equivalent to splitting the associated exact sequence of groups. Indeed, since
\varphi
is exact, a section of
M^{\rm gp}\to N^{\rm gp}
base changes along
N\to N^{\rm gp}
to a map
N\to N\times_{N^{\rm gp}}M^{\rm gp}=M
which is a section of
\varphi
.

2.2.
Finite presentation up to saturation

Let
f\colon P\to Q
be a homomorphism of monoids. A
generating set
for
Q
over
P
is a subset
S\subseteq Q
for which the induced map of
P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S}\to Q
is surjective. We say that
f
is of
finite type
(or that
Q
is finitely generated over
P
) if
Q
admits a finite generating set over
P
.

A
congruence

E
on a monoid
M
is a submonoid
E\subseteq M\times M
which is also an equivalence relation. In this case, the set of equivalence classes
M/E
is a monoid and the projection
M\to M/E
is a monoid homomorphism. For a morphism of monoids
\vartheta\colon M\to N
, the fibre product
E=M\times_{N}M
is a congruence, called the
kernel congruence
of
\vartheta
. If
M\to N
is a surjective monoid homomorphism, then
N\simeq M/E
where
E
is the kernel congruence. For a subset
R\subseteq M\times M
(called a set of
relations
), the
congruence generated by

R
is the smallest congruence
E\subseteq M\times M
containing
R
. We write
M/R
for
M/E
and call it the
quotient of
M
by the set of relations
R
. The morphism
M\to M/R
is initial among the maps
f\colon M\to N
with the property that
f(a)=f(b)
for every
(a,b)\in R
.

For a monoid homomorphism
f\colon P\to Q
, a
presentation

(S,R)
of
Q
over
P
consists of a generating set
S\subseteq Q
and a set of relations
R\subseteq(P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S})^{2}
such that the induced map
P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S}\to Q
identifies
Q
with the quotient
(P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S})/R
. We say that
f
is of
finite presentation
(or that
Q
is finitely presented over
P
) if
Q
admits a presentation
(S,R)
where both
S
and
R
are finite (see
[
AR94
, §3.10]
for a general notion of a finitely presented object in any algebraic theory).

A monoid
P
is
finitely generated
(resp.
finitely presented
) if the map
0\to P
is of finite type (resp. of finite presentation).

Notation 2.2.1
.

We denote by
\mathbf{Sat}_{P/}
the category of saturated monoids over the monoid
P
, and by
\mathbf{Mon}_{P/}
the category of monoids over
P
. Here a monoid over
P
is a morphism
P\to Q
of monoids, which is an object under
P
in the categorical sense.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

A morphism
P\to Q
of monoids is of finite presentation if and only if
Q
is a compact object of
\mathbf{Mon}_{P/}
.

Proof.

This is
[
AR94
, Corollary 3.13]
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.3
.

Base change (pushout in the category of monoids) preserves finite type (resp. finite presentation).

Proof.

Let
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
be a morphism of monoids and let
P\to Q
be the base change along
P_{0}\to P
. If
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
is of finite type generated by a finite set
S\subseteq Q_{0}
, then
Q
is also finitely generated over
P
by the image of
S
under the map
Q_{0}\to Q
.

Let now
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
be of finite presentation. By
Lemma

2.2.2
we must show that the pushout along
P_{0}\to P
of a compact object in
\mathbf{Mon}_{P_{0}/}
is a compact object in
\mathbf{Mon}_{P/}
.
But this is formal since base change preserves compact objects: for any filtered colimit
T=\varinjlim_{i}T_{i}
of monoids over
P
, we have

\Hom_{P}(Q,T)=\Hom_{P_{0}}(Q_{0},T)=\varinjlim_{i}\Hom_{P_{0}}(Q_{0},T_{i})=\varinjlim_{i}\Hom_{P}(Q,T_{i}).\qed

Lemma 2.2.4
.

Any morphism
P\to Q
between finitely presented monoids is of finite presentation.

Proof.

By
Lemma

2.2.2
it is equivalent to show that a morphism
P\to Q
of compact objects of the category of monoids is a compact object in the category
\mathbf{Mon}_{P/}
of monoids over
P
. But this is again just formal.
∎

Proposition 2.2.5
.

Let
P\to Q
be a morphism of monoids.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

The morphism
P\to Q
is of finite presentation.

(2)

The monoid
Q
is a compact object of
\mathbf{Mon}_{P/}
.

(3)

There exists a pushout square in the category of monoids

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}

where
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
are finitely presented monoids.

Proof.

We already cited
[
AR94
, Corollary 3.13]
for the equivalence
1

\Leftrightarrow

2
.

3

\Rightarrow

1
:
the morphism
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
is finitely presented by
Lemma

2.2.4
. Then the base change is finitely presented by
Lemma

2.2.3
.

1

\Rightarrow

3
:
Let
S\subseteq Q
be a finite generating set of
Q
over
P
such that that the congruence
E\subseteq(P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S})^{2}
defining
Q
as a quotient of
P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S}

is generated by the finite set
R\subseteq E
.
Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\colon(P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S})^{2}\to P
denote the two projections, and let
T=\pi_{1}(R)\cup\pi_{2}(R)
be the set of elements that occur as
P
-components of elements in tuples in
R

Define
P_{0}={\mathds{N}}^{T}
and consider
T
as a subset of
{\mathds{N}}^{T}
as the set of generators. Each element of
R
has a unique preimage in
(T\times{\mathds{N}}^{S})^{2}
under the map induced by
P_{0}\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S}\to P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S}
that maps a generator of
{\mathds{N}}^{T}=P_{0}
to the respective element in
P
. Let
R_{0}
be the union of these preimages, so that
R_{0}
tautologically lifts
R
.
Define further the quotient

Q_{0}=(P_{0}\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S})/R_{0}.

Then there is a commutative square as in the claim
3

induced by the morphism
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
and the inclusions
T\to P
and
S\to Q
. The square is a pushout square by construction and the definition of finite presentation. This shows
3
since
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
are finitely presented by construction. ∎

Definition 2.2.6
.

Let
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
be a morphism of saturated monoids.

(1)

A
sat-generating set
for
Q
over
P
is a subset
S\subseteq Q
such that
Q
equals the saturation in
Q^{\rm gp}
of the sub-monoid generated by
S
and the image of
P
.

(2)

We say that
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
is
of finite type up to saturation (sft)
if it admits a finite sat-generating set, or equivalently if there exists a factorisation

(2.2.1)

P\longrightarrow Q^{\prime}\longrightarrow Q

where the map
P\to Q^{\prime}
is of finite type and the map
Q^{\prime}\to Q
induces an isomorphism
(Q^{\prime})^{\rm sat}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}Q
.

(3)

A
sat-presentation
for
Q
over
P
is a pair
(S,R)
where
S\subseteq Q
is a sat-generating set and
R\subseteq(P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S})^{2}
is a subset such that
Q^{\prime}=P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S}/R
fits into a factorization
2.2.1
where again
(Q^{\prime})^{\rm sat}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}Q
(we do not assume that
Q^{\prime}\to Q
is injective).

(4)

We say that
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
is
of finite presentation up to saturation (sfp)
if it admits a sat-presentation
(S,R)
where both
S
and
R
are finite, or equivalently if there exists a factorisation
2.2.1
where
P\to Q^{\prime}
is of finite presentation.

Remark 2.2.7
.

It is not true that every sfp morphism is of finite type, see
Example

ex:typeV-not-fg
2
and
Example

ex:typeV-not-fg
3
, where the morphisms are even Kummer étale (
Definition

2.4.4
).

Lemma 2.2.8
.

The saturated base change of an sft (resp. sfp) morphism is again sft (resp. sfp).

Proof.

This follows from the definition of sft (resp. sfp) maps,
Lemma

2.2.3
and the fact that saturation commutes with (saturated) pushouts.
∎

We first characterise sfp morphisms with source an fs monoid.

Proposition 2.2.9
.

Let
P\to Q
be a morphism of saturated monoids with
P
an fs monoid. The following are equivalent:

(1)

The monoid
Q
is fs.

(2)

The morphism
P\to Q
is sft.

(3)

The morphism
P\to Q
is of finite type.

(4)

The morphism
P\to Q
is sfp.

(5)

The morphism
P\to Q
is of finite presentation.

Proof.

1

\Rightarrow

5
:
By
[
Ogu18
, Theorem I 2.1.7]
, every fs monoid is finitely presented. Assertion
5
follows from
Lemma

2.2.4
.

The implications

5

\Rightarrow

4

\Rightarrow

2
and
5

\Rightarrow

3

\Rightarrow

2
are trivial. So we are left to prove the following.

2

\Rightarrow

1
:
Pick a factorisation
P\to Q^{\prime}\to Q
with
P\to Q^{\prime}
finitely generated and
Q^{\prime}\to Q
an isomorphism after saturation. As an fs monoid,
P
is finitely generated, hence
Q^{\prime}
is finitely generated. Then
Q
is fs, being the saturation of a finitely generated monoid by Gordan’s Lemma (cf.
[
Sti02
, Lemma 3.1.1]
).
∎

For general sfp morphisms, we have the following important characterisations.

Proposition 2.2.10
.

Let
P\to Q
be a morphism of saturated monoids.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

The morphism
P\to Q
is sfp.

(2)

The monoid
Q
is a compact object of
\mathbf{Sat}_{P/}
.

(3)

There exists a pushout square in the category of saturated monoids

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}

(so
Q=(P\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0})^{\rm sat}
) where
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
is a morphism of fs monoids.

The above conditions
1
–
3
further imply and, if
P\to Q
is injective, are equivalent to the following condition:

(4)

The morphism
P\to Q
is sft.

Proof.

1

\Rightarrow

3
:
Let
P\to Q^{\prime}\to Q
be a factorisation such that
P\to Q^{\prime}
is finitely presented and
(Q^{\prime})^{\rm sat}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}Q
is an isomorphism. By
Proposition

2.2.5
we find finitely presented monoids
P^{\prime}_{0}\to Q^{\prime}_{0}
and a map
P^{\prime}_{0}\to P
such that
Q^{\prime}=P\oplus_{P^{\prime}_{0}}Q^{\prime}_{0}
. The map
P_{0}=(P^{\prime}_{0})^{\rm sat}\to Q_{0}=(Q^{\prime}_{0})^{\rm sat}
is a map of fs monoids by Gordan’s Lemma.
Since saturation commutes with pushout, we find
Q=(Q^{\prime})^{\rm sat}=(P\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0})^{\rm sat}
as requested.

3

\Rightarrow

2
:
By
Proposition

2.2.9
the morphism
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
in the diagram in
3
is finitely presented. Hence
Q_{0}
is a compact object in
\mathbf{Mon}_{P_{0}/}
by
Lemma

2.2.2
. Since the inclusion
\mathbf{Sat}_{P_{0}/}\to\mathbf{Mon}_{P_{0}/}
preserves filtered colimits, its left adjoint functor
saturation
preserves compact objects. Therefore the fs monoid
Q_{0}
is a compact object in
\mathbf{Sat}_{P_{0}/}
. Base change along
P_{0}\to P
preserves compact objects and so shows
2
.

Although
1

\Rightarrow

4
is obvious, we include a proof of
2

\Rightarrow

4
because this serves as the first part of our proof of
2

\Rightarrow

1
.

2

\Rightarrow

4
:
For any finite set
S\subseteq Q
we denote by
Q(S)_{0}
the submonoid of
Q
generated by
S
over
P
, and by
Q(S)=Q(S)_{0}^{\rm sat}
its saturation. Note that
Q(S)_{0}
is integral by definition and that
Q(S)\to Q
is injective. Then clearly

Q=\varinjlim_{S}Q(S)

as a colimit of saturations of monoids finitely generated over
P
. As
P\to Q
is a compact object, we have

\Hom_{P}(Q,\varinjlim_{S}Q(S))=\varinjlim_{S}\Hom_{P}(Q,Q(S)).

Therefore, the identity
\id_{Q}
lifts to a morphism
Q\to Q(S)
for large enough
S
. It follows that the injective map
Q(S)\to Q
is an isomorphism, i.e.
S
sat-generates
Q
over
P
.

2

\Rightarrow

1
:
We already know that
P\to Q
is sft by the proof of
2

\Rightarrow

4
above.
Pick any finite sat-generating set
S
of
Q
over
P
, and consider the kernel congruence
E\subseteq(P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S})^{2}
of the morphism

P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S}\to Q,\qquad(p,n)\mapsto p+\sum_{s\in S}n_{s}s.

with image
Q_{0}=(P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S})/E
.
For any finite subset
R\subseteq E
we denote by
Q_{R}
the saturation of the quotient

Q_{R,0}=(P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S})/R.

It follows that

\varinjlim_{R}Q_{R}=\big(\varinjlim_{R}Q_{R,0}\big)^{\rm sat}\to Q_{0}^{\rm sat}=Q

is an isomorphism over
P
. Again, by
Q
being a compact object over
P
, we have

\Hom_{P}(Q,\varinjlim_{R}Q_{R})=\varinjlim_{R}\Hom_{P}(Q,Q_{R})

so again the identity
\id_{Q}
lifts to a map
\iota\colon Q\to Q_{R}
.

As for
R\subseteq R^{\prime}
the map
Q_{R,0}\twoheadrightarrow Q_{R^{\prime},0}
is surjective, also the map
Q_{R}^{\rm gp}\twoheadrightarrow Q_{R^{\prime}}^{\rm gp}
is surjective.
The filtered colimit of finitely generated abelian groups and surjective transition maps

\varinjlim_{R}(Q_{R}^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp})=(\varinjlim_{R}Q_{R})^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}=Q^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}

becomes constant for
R\gg 0
, because
{\mathds{Z}}
is a noetherian ring.

We choose
R
large enough so that
both properties hold:
Q_{R}^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}Q^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}
is an isomorphism and
\id_{Q}
lifts to
\iota\colon Q\to Q_{R}
over
P
, i.e. splitting the natural map
r\colon Q_{R}\to Q
. The restrictions of
r^{\rm gp}
and
\iota^{\rm gp}
to the
images of
P^{\rm gp}\to Q^{\rm gp}
and
P^{\rm gp}\to Q_{R}^{\rm gp}
are mutually inverse isomorphisms (since
\iota^{\rm gp}
is injective). By the
5
-lemma we conclude that
r^{\rm gp}\colon Q_{R}^{\rm gp}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}Q^{\rm gp}
is an isomorphism and this shows that the map
r\colon Q_{R}\to Q
is injective. As a retract map,
r
is also surjective. Now
P\to Q_{R}=Q
is sfp by construction, and this concludes the proof of
1
.

We now assume that
P\to Q
is injective.

4

\Rightarrow

1
:
Let
S
be a finite sat-generating set of
Q
over
P
. The morphism
P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S}\to Q
induces a surjection

P^{\rm gp}\oplus{\mathds{Z}}^{S}\twoheadrightarrow Q^{\rm gp}

with kernel
K
. By assumption
P\to Q
is injective, and, by
Lemma

2.1.1
, also
P^{\rm gp}\hookrightarrow Q^{\rm gp}
is injective. Hence the second projection induces an injective map
K\hookrightarrow{\mathds{Z}}^{S}
. Thus
K
is a finitely generated abelian group.

Let
f_{i}-g_{i}\in K
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
be generators of
K
with
f_{i},g_{i}\in P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S}
. Using

R=\{(f_{i},g_{i})\ ;\ i=1,\ldots,n\}

we define the monoid

Q^{\prime}=(P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{S})/R

which is finitely presented over
P
by construction. Since
Q
is saturated, the relations
R
hold in
Q
and we obtain an induced morphism
Q^{\prime}\to Q
. Then

Q^{\prime{\rm gp}}=(P^{\rm gp}\oplus{\mathds{Z}}^{S})/K\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}Q^{\rm gp}.

Therefore the natural map
(Q^{\prime})^{\rm sat}\to Q
is injective, and it is surjective because the image contains
S
. This shows that
P\to Q
is sfp.
∎

Remark 2.2.11
.

The reader may have noticed that the description of compact objects in
\mathbf{Mon}_{/P}
as the finitely presented morphisms (
Lemma

2.2.2
) was much easier to obtain than the corresponding statement about compact objects in
\mathbf{Sat}_{/P}
being precisely the sfp morphisms (
Proposition

2.2.10
). The intuitive reason for this is that saturated monoids do not form an “algebraic theory” (more precisely, an
equational class
or a
variety
in the sense of universal algebra), as the saturation axioms contain an existential quantifier. Trying to deduce the statement about compact objects in
\mathbf{Sat}_{P/}
from the one about compact objects in
\mathbf{Mon}_{P/}
using the natural adjunction between the two categories, one can completely formally obtain the following statement:
Q
is a compact object in
\mathbf{Sat}_{P/}
if and only if
Q
is a
retract
of the saturation of a finitely presented monoid over
P
. Without arguments as in last step of the proof of
Proposition

2.2.10

2
\Rightarrow
1
, employing crucially the noetherianity of
{\mathds{Z}}
, it is not obvious why we can get rid of the “retract” part.

Remark 2.2.12
.

The proof for
4

\Rightarrow

1
in

Proposition

2.2.10
shares similarities with the proof of
[
ALPT19
, Lemma 2.1.7]
: an injective homomorphism
P\to Q
of integral monoids which is of finite type (or, equivalently, of finite type up to integralisation) is of finite presentation up to integralisation (ifp). This is an instance of the parallel story of notions based on integral monoids and integralisation rather than saturated monoids and saturation that we remarked in a footnote in the introduction.

Corollary 2.2.13
.

The composition of sfp morphisms is again sfp.

Proof.

Let
P\to Q
and
Q\to R
be sfp morphisms. Then by
Proposition

prop:sfp-conditions
2

R
is a compact object in
\mathbf{Sat}_{Q/}
and
Q
is a compact object in
\mathbf{Sat}_{P/}
. It is formal that then
R
is a compact object in
\mathbf{Sat}_{P/}
and hence
P\to R
is an sfp morphism by
Proposition

2.2.10
.
∎

Corollary 2.2.14
.

The fs monoids are the compact objects of the category of saturated monoids.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.2.10
, a compact object is the same as a monoid
P
with
0\to P
an sfp morphism. These are precisely the fs monoids.
∎

Corollary 2.2.15
.

Let
P=\varinjlim_{i\in I}P_{i}
be a filtered colimit of saturated monoids.

(1)

Let
0\in I
and let
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
and
P_{0}\to R_{0}
be two sfp morphisms. Denote by
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
,
P_{i}\to R_{i}
(
i\geq 0
),
P\to Q
,
P\to R
the respective saturated base changes. Then the map

\varinjlim_{i\geq 0}\Hom_{P_{i}}(Q_{i},R_{i})\longrightarrow\Hom_{P}(Q,R)

is bijective.

(2)

For every sfp morphism
P\to Q
, there exists an index
i\in I
and an sfp morphism
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
fitting into a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{i}}

in the category of saturated monoids.

The above statement can be rephrased as follows. Let
\mathbf{Sfp}_{P}
denote the category of sfp morphisms
P\to Q
. Then saturated base change induces an equivalence of categories

\varinjlim\mathbf{Sfp}_{P_{i}}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}\mathbf{Sfp}_{P}.

For a variant of this result for integral monoids see
[
ALPT19
, Lemma 2.1.9]
.

Proof.

1
:
We compute

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{i\geq 0}\Hom_{P_{i}}(Q_{i},R_{i})

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{i\geq 0}\Hom_{P_{0}}(Q_{0},R_{i})

\displaystyle=\Hom_{P_{0}}(Q_{0},\varinjlim_{i\geq 0}R_{i})=\Hom_{P_{0}}(Q_{0},R)=\Hom_{P}(Q,R)

because
Q_{0}
is a compact object
in
\mathbf{Sfp}_{P_{0}}
by
Proposition

prop:sfp-conditions
2
.

2
:
By
Proposition

2.2.10
we can find a pushout square in the category of saturated monoids

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}

where
P^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime}
is a morphism of fs monoids.
Since
P^{\prime}
is a compact object in the category of saturated monoids by
Corollary

2.2.14
, there is an index
i\in I
such that
P^{\prime}\to P
factors through
P^{\prime}\to P_{i}
.
We denote by
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
the saturated base change of
P^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime}
to
P_{i}
. By
Proposition

2.2.9
the map
P^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime}
is sfp, and
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
is sfp by
Lemma

2.2.8
. This gives the desired pushout square.
∎

2.3.
Faces and prime ideals

An
ideal
of a monoid
P
is a subset
K\subseteq P
such that
P+K=K
. It is
prime
if
K\neq P
and if
p+p^{\prime}\in K
implies that either
p\in K
or
p^{\prime}\in K
. The set of prime ideals of
P
is denoted by
\Spec(P)
. The topology generated by the sets

\{K\in\Spec(P)\ |\ x\notin K\}\qquad(x\in P)

makes it into a
T_{0}
-space with generic point
\emptyset
and unique closed point
P\setminus P^{\times}
.

A
face
of
P
is a submonoid
F\subseteq P
such that
p+p^{\prime}\in F
implies that
p\in F
and
p^{\prime}\in F
. Faces of
P
are precisely the complements of its prime ideals. We will often identify
\Spec(P)
with the set of faces of
P
.

For a face
F\subseteq P
of an integral monoid, we define the localization
P_{F}
of
P
at
F
as

P_{F}=P+F^{\rm gp}\quad\subseteq P^{\rm gp},

and the quotient
P/F
as the quotient monoid

P/F=P_{F}/F^{\rm gp}=\im(P_{F}\to P^{\rm gp}/F^{\rm gp}).

We say that a face
F
of
P
is
generated as a face
by a subset
S\subseteq F
if it is the intersection of all faces containing
S
. In case
S
is finite, we may replace
S
with the sum
s
of all of its elements, and then the face generated by
S
is

F=\{x\in P\ |\ \exists{y\in P},\ \exists{n\geq 1}:x+y=ns\}.

Remark 2.3.1
.

For an integral monoid
P
, the saturation map
P\to P^{\rm sat}
induces a bijection
\Spec(P^{\rm sat})\to\Spec(P)
. The inverse assigns to a face
F
of
P
the face generated by
F
in
P^{\rm sat}
.

Lemma 2.3.2
.

Let
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
be a homomorphism of saturated monoids, let
F
be a face of
Q
, and let
S\subseteq Q
be a sat-generating set relative to
P
. Then,
F
equals the saturation of the submonoid of
Q
generated by
F\cap\vartheta(P)
and
F\cap S
.

In particular, if
P\to Q
is sft, then the map of faces
\vartheta^{-1}(F)\to F
is sft.

Proof.

By definition of a sat-generating set, for an element
q\in Q
, there exists an
n\geq 1
and an equation of the form

nq=\vartheta(p)+\sum_{s\in S}m_{s}\cdot s

for some
p\in P
and a function
m\colon S\to{\mathds{N}}
with finite support. Suppose that
q\in F
. By definition of a face, it follows that
\vartheta(p)\in F
and
s\in F
for every
s\in S
such that
m_{s}>0
. We thus have written
nq
as a combination of elements in
F\cap\vartheta(P)
and
F\cap S
.
∎

Corollary 2.3.3
.

Let
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
be an sft map. Then the fibres of the map
\Spec(Q)\to\Spec(P)
are finite with cardinality bounded by
2^{\#S}
with the size
\#S
of any sat-generating set
S
relative to
P
. In particular, if
P
has finitely many faces, then so does
Q
.

Proof.

Lemma

2.3.2
shows that faces
F\subseteq Q
with given face
\vartheta^{-1}(F)
are uniquely determined by the subset
F\cap S
.
∎

2.4.
Elementary studies of certain classes of morphisms

We first recall the definition of some classical notions for morphisms of monoids.

Definition 2.4.1
.

A homomorphism
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
of integral monoids is

(1)

exact

[
Ogu18
, Definition I 2.1.15]
if the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\rm gp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\rm gp}}

is cartesian, or equivalently if
P=(\vartheta^{\rm gp})^{-1}(Q)
,

(2)

integral

[
Ogu18
, Definition I 4.6.2]
if for every homomorphism
P\to P^{\prime}
into an integral monoid
P^{\prime}
, we have

P^{\prime}\oplus_{P}Q=(P^{\prime}\oplus_{P}Q)^{\rm int},

(3)

saturated

[
Tsu19
, Definition 3.12 and Proposition 3.14]

if
P\to Q
is integral,
P
and
Q
are saturated, and for every homomorphism
P\to P^{\prime}
into a saturated monoid
P^{\prime}
, we have

P^{\prime}\oplus_{P}Q=(P^{\prime}\oplus_{P}Q)^{\rm sat},

(4)

vertical
if the image of
P
generates
Q
as a face: for every
q\in Q
there exist
q^{\prime}\in Q
and
p\in P
such that
q+q^{\prime}=\vartheta(p)
, or equivalently if
Q
and
-\vartheta(P)
generate
Q^{\rm gp}
.

Remarks 2.4.2
.

(1)

An often useful fact about exact morphisms
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
is that providing a section of
\vartheta
is equivalent to providing a section of

\vartheta^{\rm gp}\colon P^{\rm gp}\to Q^{\rm gp}
.

(2)

The class of exact morphisms is closed under composition, integral pushout and, if
P
and
Q
are saturated, saturated pushout

[
Ogu18
, Proposition I 4.2.1]
.

(3)

The class of integral (resp. saturated) morphisms is closed under composition, integral (resp. saturated) pushout.

(4)

The class of vertical morphisms is closed under composition and integral pushout. Moreover, if
P
and
Q
are saturated, then being vertical is also stable under saturated pushout
[
Ogu18
, Proposition I 4.3.3]
.

Example 2.4.3
.

The class of integral morphisms is in general not preserved by saturated pushout. We learned this from Arthur Ogus with essentially the following example. Consider the morphism
\vartheta_{0}\colon{\mathds{N}}^{2}\to\frac{1}{2}{\mathds{N}}^{2}
which is integral e.g. by
[
Ogu18
, Proposition I 4.6.7]
. Let
P\subseteq\frac{1}{2}{\mathds{N}}^{2}
be the submonoid generated by
{\mathds{N}}^{2}
and
(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
. Consider the saturated base change

\vartheta\colon P\longrightarrow Q=\left(P\oplus_{{\mathds{N}}^{2}}{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{\mathds{N}}^{2}\right)^{\rm sat}

of
\vartheta_{0}
along the inclusion
{\mathds{N}}^{2}\to P
. Then
Q\simeq\frac{1}{2}{\mathds{N}}^{2}\times{\mathds{Z}}/2{\mathds{Z}}
with
\vartheta(P)
contained in the first factor. The morphism
\vartheta
is not integral as can easily be verified by computing the base change in monoids with the “sum of the coordinates” map
P\to\frac{1}{2}{\mathds{N}}
.

We shall now use the notion of an sfp map to define smooth, étale, and Kummer étale maps between saturated monoids. These definitions are motivated by the classical notions in log geometry.

Definition 2.4.4
.

Henceforth, we fix a possibly empty set of primes
\Sigma
and set

p=\prod_{\ell\in\Sigma}\ell

as a supernatural number (formal product). In the applications the set
\Sigma
will be clear out of context, so we drop it in the notation.

A morphism of saturated monoids
P\to Q
is

(1)

smooth
if it is sfp and the kernel and the torsion part of the cokernel of
P^{\rm gp}\to Q^{\rm gp}
are finite groups of order prime to
p
,

(2)

étale
if it is smooth and the cokernel of
P^{\rm gp}\to Q^{\rm gp}
is torsion,

(3)

Kummer étale
if it is injective, étale, and exact.

Example 2.4.5

(“Semistable reduction”)
.

Let
P
be a saturated monoid, let
\pi\in P
, and let
n\geq 0
. Define

P_{n}(\pi)=P[e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}]/(e_{1}+\cdots+e_{n}=\pi)

as the quotient of
P\oplus{\mathds{N}}^{n}
by the congruence relation generated by
(\pi,0)\sim(0,(1,\ldots,1))
. Then
P_{n}(\pi)
is saturated and
P\to P_{n}(\pi)
is smooth, vertical, and saturated. In the case
P={\mathds{N}}
and
\pi=1
, these assertions are classical: the first three follow directly from the definitions. That the morphism is saturated follows from the characterization of
[
Tsu19
, Theorem I.6.3(8)]
(use
[
Tsu19
, Theorem I.2.1]
first to check that the morphism is integral): for any field
k
, the morphism

{\mathds{A}}_{P_{n}[\pi]}=\Spec(k[t][x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/(x_{1}\cdot\ldots x_{n}-t))\longrightarrow\Spec(k[t])={\mathds{A}}_{{\mathds{N}}}

is flat and has reduced special fibre. The general case follows by base change along the map
{\mathds{N}}\to P
sending
1
to
\pi
, see
[
Ogu18
, Example IV 3.1.17]
.

Lemma 2.4.6
.

Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be one of the properties:
smooth, étale, injective, Kummer étale.
Then the saturated base change of a morphism with property
{\mathcal{P}}
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
.

Proof.

Let
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
and
P_{0}\to P
be morphisms of saturated monoids. We set

Q=(P\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0})^{\rm sat}

for the saturated pushout. Recall from
Lemma

2.1.2
that
Q_{0}^{\rm gp}/P_{0}^{\rm gp}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}Q^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}
is an isomorphism and the morphism

\ker(P_{0}^{\rm gp}\to Q_{0}^{\rm gp})\twoheadrightarrow\ker(P^{\rm gp}\to Q^{\rm gp})
is surjective.

As being sfp is stable under saturated base change by
Lemma

2.2.8
, clearly smooth and étale are also stable under saturated base change. The claim for injective follows because by
Lemma

2.1.1
, the map
P\to Q
is injective if and only if
P^{\rm gp}\to Q^{\rm gp}
is injective. Now Kummer étale follows as well being defined as the combination of base change stable properties (for “exact” see Remark
2.4.2
2
).
∎

Proposition 2.4.7
.

Let
P\to Q
be an sfp morphism of saturated monoids and let
{\mathcal{P}}
be one of the following properties: smooth, étale, injective, exact, vertical, Kummer étale. Then
P\to Q
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
if and only if there exists a morphism of fs monoids
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
with property
{\mathcal{P}}
and a map
P_{0}\to P
such that
Q=(P\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0})^{\rm sat}
.

Proof.

If such a map
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
with property
{\mathcal{P}}
as in the lemma exists, then
P\to Q
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
by
Remarks

2.4.2
and
Lemma

2.4.6
.

For the converse direction we assume that
P\to Q
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
. Let
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
be a morphism of fs monoids with
Q=(P\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0})^{\rm sat}
as in
Proposition

prop:sfp-conditions
3
. We write
P=\varinjlim_{i}P_{i}
as a filtered colimit of fs monoids over
P_{0}
, namely the saturations of submonoids that are finitely generated over
P_{0}
. We set
Q_{i}=(P_{i}\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0})^{\rm sat}
which yields
Q=\varinjlim_{i}Q_{i}
as a filtered colimit of fs monoids. Moreover, by possibly replacing
P_{0}
by one of the
P_{i}
and the system by all
P_{j}
with
j\geq i
, we may and do assume that all
P_{i}
are submonoids of
P
: the transfer maps in
P=\varinjlim_{i}P_{i}
are injective. We abbreviate

K_{i}=\ker(P_{i}^{\rm gp}\to Q_{i}^{\rm gp})\quad\text{ and }\quad C_{i}=\coker(P_{i}^{\rm gp}\to Q_{i}^{\rm gp}).

For
j\geq i
, transitivity of pushouts yields
Q_{j}=(P_{j}\oplus_{P_{i}}Q_{i})^{\rm sat}
.
We apply
Lemma

2.1.2
and obtain
isomorphisms
C_{i}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}C_{j}
and
K_{i}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}K_{j}
because by construction
P_{i}^{\rm gp}\to P_{j}^{\rm gp}
is injective as subgroups of
P^{\rm gp}
.

Since groupification commutes with colimits and filtered colimits are exact, we find an exact complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{i}K_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\rm gp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\rm gp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{i}C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

This shows that all maps
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
and
P\to Q
have isomorphic kernels and cokernels of their groupification. Being sfp
as maps between fs monoids by
Proposition

prop:sfp-conditions
3
, we deduce that
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
for
{\mathcal{P}}
equal to: smooth, étale, injective. It remains to argue for
{\mathcal{P}}
equal to exact and
{\mathcal{P}}
equal to vertical.

Suppose that
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
is exact. For all
i
we set
P_{i}^{\prime}=\smash{P_{i}^{\rm gp}\times_{Q_{i}^{\rm gp}}Q_{i}}
. This is an fs monoid, being the preimage of the fs monoid
Q_{i}
under the homomorphism of finitely generated abelian groups
P_{i}^{\rm gp}\to Q_{i}^{\rm gp}
. Moreover, since
P\to Q
is exact, we obtain the factorisation
P_{i}\to P_{i}^{\prime}\to P
. In the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{i}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{i}}

both the outer rectangle and the right square are pushout diagrams of saturated monoids. Indeed, the saturated pushout

P^{\prime}_{i}\oplus^{\rm sat}_{P_{i}}Q_{i}
is the saturation in
Q_{i}^{\rm gp}={P^{\prime}_{i}}^{\rm gp}\oplus_{P^{\rm gp}_{i}}Q_{i}^{\rm gp}
of the submonoid generated by
Q_{i}
and
P_{i}^{\prime}
, and this is still only
Q_{i}
. Hence also the left square is a pushout diagram of saturated monoids. So
\vartheta
is the saturated base change of the exact map
\vartheta_{i}^{\prime}\colon P_{i}^{\prime}\to Q_{i}
.

Finally, suppose that
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
is vertical. Let
S
be a finite sat-generating set for
Q
over
P
. Then
\vartheta
is vertical if and only if for every
s\in S
there exist
q_{s}\in Q
and
p_{s}\in P
such that

s+q_{s}=\vartheta(p_{s}).

We may assume that
S
is the image of a finite sat-generating set
S_{i}
of
Q_{i}
over
P_{i}
. Increasing
i
, we may ensure the existence of elements
q^{\prime}_{r}\in Q_{i}
and
p^{\prime}_{r}\in P_{i}
(for
r\in S_{i}
) such that
r+q^{\prime}_{r}=\vartheta_{i}(p^{\prime}_{r})
. This implies that
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
is vertical.
∎

Combining this with
Corollary

2.2.15
, we obtain the following results.

Proposition 2.4.8
.

Let
P=\varinjlim_{i\in I}P_{i}
be a filtered colimit of saturated monoids, let
0\in I
be the smallest element, let
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
be an sfp map, and let
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
(
i\geq 0
) and
P\to Q
be obtained by saturated base change. Then
P\to Q
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
(as in
Proposition

2.4.7
) if and only if
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
for
i\gg 0
.

Proof.

The “if” part follows from
Remarks

2.4.2
and
Lemma

2.4.6
. For the converse, suppose that
P\to Q
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
. By
Proposition

2.4.7
, we find a cocartesian square in the category of saturated monoids

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}

with
P^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime}
a morphism of fs monoids which has property
{\mathcal{P}}
. Since
P^{\prime}
is a compact object in the category of saturated monoids, the map
P^{\prime}\to P
factors through
P_{i_{0}}
for
i_{0}\gg 0
. Fix such a factorisation, and for
i\geq i_{0}
, let
Q^{\prime}_{i}=(Q^{\prime}\oplus_{P^{\prime}}P_{i})^{\rm sat}
. Then by
Remarks

2.4.2
and
Lemma

2.4.6
, the maps
P_{i}\to Q^{\prime}_{i}
have property
{\mathcal{P}}
. By
Corollary

2.2.15
, for
i\gg i_{0}
we have
Q_{i}\simeq Q^{\prime}_{i}
over
P_{i}
, and hence for such
i
, the map
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
has property
{\mathcal{P}}
as well.
∎

Corollary 2.4.9
.

In the situation of
Proposition

2.4.8
, the category of smooth, étale, or Kummer étale maps
P\to Q
is equivalent to the filtered colimit of the categories of maps
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
with the same property.

2.5.
Monoids of type
(\mathrm{V})
and of type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})

Using the notion of an sfp map, we shall now define monoids of type
(\mathrm{V})
(resp. type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
) as those which are sfp over a valuative monoid (resp. divisible valuative monoid). The two assumptions valuative and divisible for the base monoid enable us to apply the results of F. Kato (
Theorem

2.5.6
) and T. Tsuji (
Theorem

2.5.14
).

Valuative monoids

Recall that a commutative monoid
V
is
valuative
if it is integral and for every
x\in V^{\rm gp}
, either
x\in V
or
-x\in V
, see
[
Ogu18
, I §1.3]
. For example, if
K^{+}
is a valuation ring with fraction field
K
, then the monoid
K^{+}\setminus\{0\}
as well as the monoid
\Gamma_{K}^{+}=(K^{+}\setminus\{0\})/(K^{+})^{\times}
of non-negative elements in its value group
\Gamma_{K}=K^{\times}/(K^{+})^{\times}
are valuative monoids. If
\Gamma
is a totally ordered abelian group, then its submonoid
\Gamma^{+}
of non-negative elements is sharp and valuative, and conversely the groupification
V^{\rm gp}
of a sharp valuative monoid
V
is totally ordered by
x\geq y
if
x-y\in V
. A valuative monoid is automatically saturated, but it will typically not be finitely generated (the only sharp valuative fs monoids are
0
and
{\mathds{N}}
).

Definition, basic properties, and examples

We will call a saturated monoid
P

divisible
if for every
n\geq 1
, the multiplication by
n
map
n\colon P\to P
is surjective, or equivalently if
P^{\rm gp}
is a divisible group.

Definition 2.5.1
.

A saturated monoid
Q
is of
type
(\mathrm{V})
(resp.
of type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
) if there exists a valuative monoid
V
(resp. a divisible valuative monoid
V
) and an sfp map
V\to Q
.

Trivially, if
Q\to Q^{\prime}
is an sfp map and
Q
is of type
(\mathrm{V})
or
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
then so is
Q^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.5.2
.

Every homomorphism from a valuative monoid
V
to an integral monoid
Q
is integral by
[
Ogu18
, Proposition I 4.6.3(5)]
. In particular, any
V\to Q
as in
Definition

2.5.1
is integral as well.

Example 2.5.3
.

We illustrate the notion of a monoid of type
(\mathrm{V})
, especially the flexibility arising since the monoid
V
itself is not fixed as part of the structure.

(1)

Every fs monoid is of type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
, simply over the trivial valuative monoid
V=0
.

(2)

It is not true in general that if
V\to Q
is sfp and
V
is valuative then
Q
is finitely generated over
V
. For example, let
V=({\mathds{Z}}\oplus{\mathds{Z}})^{+}
be the non-negative elements in
{\mathds{Z}}\oplus{\mathds{Z}}
with lexicographical ordering, i.e.

V=\left({\mathds{N}}_{>0}\times{\mathds{Z}}\right)+\left(0\times{\mathds{N}}\right)\quad\subseteq\quad{\mathds{N}}\times{\mathds{Z}}\quad\subseteq\quad V^{\rm gp}={\mathds{Z}}^{2}

and let

Q={\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}V\subseteq{\mathds{Q}}^{2}

(see
Fig.

1
, in which the smaller dots are in
Q
and the larger ones are in
V
). Then
Q
is sat-generated over
V
by
\{(\frac{1}{2},0),(0,\frac{1}{2})\}
,
and hence sft and even sfp by
Proposition

2.2.10
. Even more,
V\to Q
is Kummer étale (for
\Sigma=\{2\}
). But any set of generators of
Q
over
V
has to contain an infinite subset of the elements
\frac{1}{2}\times(-{\mathds{N}})
.
However, in this example
Q
is finitely generated over a valuative monoid (namely, over itself), so in particular
Q
is of type
(\mathrm{V})
. See
Example

4.4.6
for a related example in log schemes.

(3)

For a similar example with a valuative monoid of rank one, let
\lambda
be an irrational real number and let

V=\{(a,b)\in{\mathds{Z}}^{2}\,:\,a+b\lambda\geq 0\},

which is identified with a dense submonoid of
{\mathds{R}}^{+}
via
(a,b)\mapsto a+b\lambda
. Again, set
Q=\frac{1}{2}V\subseteq{\mathds{Q}}^{2}
(see
Fig.

2
, in which the smaller dots are in
Q
and the larger ones are in
V
). Then
Q
is not finitely generated over
V
. Indeed, let
S\subseteq Q
be a finite subset, and let
\varepsilon=\inf\{a+b\lambda\,:\,(a,b)\in S\setminus\{0\}\}>0
. Since
\lambda
is irrational, there exists an element
(a,b)\in Q
such that
0<a+b\lambda<\varepsilon
. Dividing
a
and
b
by a power of two, we may assume that
(a,b)\notin V
. But then
(a,b)
does not belong to the submonoid of
Q
generated by
V
and
S
. We thank Jakub Byszewski for this argument. See
Example

4.4.7
for a related example in log schemes.

Figure 1.
The inclusion
V\subseteq Q
in
Example

ex:typeV-not-fg
2
.

Figure 2.
The inclusion
V\subseteq Q
in
Example

ex:typeV-not-fg
3
.

(4)

In the next example we show that in general a monoid
Q
of type
(\mathrm{V})
need not be finitely generated over a valuative monoid.
Consider again
V=({\mathds{Z}}\oplus{\mathds{Z}})^{+}
as in
2
and let
Q
be the saturation of the submonoid of
\frac{1}{2}V\oplus\frac{1}{2}{\mathds{N}}
generated by
V\oplus{\mathds{N}}
and the element
(1/2,0,1/2)
:

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\left((V\oplus{\mathds{N}})+{\mathds{N}}\cdot({\textstyle\frac{1}{2}},0,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})\right)^{\rm sat}

\displaystyle=\left\{(x,y,z)\in{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{\mathds{N}}\times{\mathds{Z}}\times{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}{\mathds{N}}\ :\ x-z\in{\mathds{Z}}\text{ and, if $y<0$, then $x>0$}\right\}.

The underlying geometric inspiration is as follows: the monoid
Q
occurs in the integral closure of the algebra
R=K^{+}[T,S]/(S^{2}-\pi T)
in
R[1/\pi]
for
K^{+}
a valuation ring with
\Gamma_{K}^{+}=V
and
\pi\in K^{+}
an element (pseudouniformizer) with valuation
(1,0)
.

Clearly
V\to Q
, the inclusion of the
xy
-plane, is sft by construction and therefore sfp by
Proposition

2.2.10
. Therefore
Q
is a monoid of type
(\mathrm{V})
.

Suppose that there is a valuative monoid
W
and a
morphism
\vartheta\colon W\to Q
, such that
Q
is finitely generated over
W
. We may replace
W
by the image of
W
in
Q
, since this image is still a valuative monoid over which
Q
is finitely generated.
Suppose that
W
is not contained in
V
, so that there exists an element
(\alpha,n)\in W\subseteq\frac{1}{2}V\oplus\frac{1}{2}{\mathds{N}}
with
n>0
. One of the elements
(-\alpha,n)
and
(\alpha,-n)
belongs to
W
. Since
n>0
, this must be
(-\alpha,n)\in W
and thus
\alpha=0
. This shows that
W\subseteq 0\times{\mathds{N}}
. Since
V
is a face of
Q
, any generating set of
Q
over
W
contains a generating set of
V
over
V\cap W=0
. But
V
is not a finitely generated monoid, a contradiction. Thus
W\subseteq V
.

Now it is enough to show that
Q
is not finitely generated over
V
. For this, we note that every generating set of
Q
over
V
must contain infinitely many of the elements

q_{n}=(1/2,-n,1/2),\quad n\in{\mathds{N}},

because every decomposition

q_{n}=a+b
in
Q
must be of the form
q_{n}=q_{n+k}+(0,k,0)
with
k\geq 0
.

(5)

Not all saturated submonoids of finitely generated groups are of type
(\mathrm{V})
. Take any saturated submonoid
Q\subseteq{\mathds{Z}}^{2}
which is not finitely generated and not valuative. Basic examples are given by

Q=({\mathds{N}}_{>0}\times{\mathds{N}})\cup\{(0,0)\}=\{(x,y)\in{\mathds{N}}^{2}\ |\ \text{$x>0$ if $y>0$}\},

or a cone with irrational slope such as

Q=\{(x,y)\in{\mathds{N}}^{2}\,:\,y\leq\sqrt{2}x\}.

It is easy to check that
Q
is saturated but not fs. If
V
is a valuative monoid and
V\to Q
is an sfp homomorphism, then the image of
V
in
Q
is a valuative monoid over which
Q
is sft (see the proof of
Proposition

2.5.8
). However, the only valuative submonoids of
Q
are of the form
{\mathds{N}}\cdot q
for some
q\in Q
, and in particular are fs. Thus, if
Q
was sft over
V
, it would be fs by
Proposition

2.2.9
.

In fact, it is very rare that a Kummer étale homomorphism between valuative monoids is of finite type. Let us call a totally ordered abelian group
\Gamma

discrete
if
\Gamma=0
or if
\Gamma^{+}\setminus\{0\}
has a smallest element
\gamma
, or equivalently if its smallest non-zero convex subgroup is isomorphic to
{\mathds{Z}}
. Now, Examples
2.5.3
2
and
2.5.3
3
can be generalized as follows.

Lemma 2.5.4
.

Let
\Gamma\to\Sigma
be an injective map of totally ordered abelian groups whose cokernel is finite (so that
\Gamma^{+}\to\Sigma^{+}
is Kummer étale with
p=1
). The following are equivalent:

(1)

\Sigma^{+}
is finite as a
\Gamma^{+}
-set,

(2)

\Sigma^{+}
is finitely generated over
\Gamma^{+}
,

(3)

either
\Gamma=\Sigma
, or
\Gamma
is discrete and there exists an integer
n\geq 1
such that
\Sigma=\Gamma+\frac{1}{n}{\mathds{Z}}\gamma
where
\gamma
is the smallest positive element of
\Gamma
.

Proof.

The implication
1
\Rightarrow
2
is clear. For
2
\Rightarrow
1
, if
S\subseteq\Sigma^{+}
is a finite generating set over
\Gamma+
, with
0\in S
, and if
n\geq 1
is an integer annihilating
\Sigma/\Gamma
, then the finite set of sums

\sum_{s\in S}m(s)\cdot s,\quad m\colon S\longrightarrow\{0,\ldots,n-1\}

generates
\Sigma^{+}
as a
\Gamma^{+}
-set. For
3
\Rightarrow
1
we note that
\Sigma^{+}
is generated as a
\Gamma^{+}
-set by the elements
\frac{i}{n}\gamma
for
0\leq i<n
. It remains to show
1
\Rightarrow
3
.

We write
\pi\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma/\Gamma
for the quotient map. For every
t\in\Sigma/\Gamma
, let
\smash{\Sigma^{+}_{t}}=\pi^{-1}(t)\cap\Sigma^{+}
be the monoid of non-negative elements of
\Sigma
congruent to
t
modulo
\Gamma
. Then
\smash{\Sigma^{+}}
decomposes as a
\Gamma^{+}
-set

\Sigma^{+}=\coprod_{t\in\Sigma/\Gamma}\Sigma^{+}_{t},

and hence
1
is equivalent to
\Sigma^{+}_{t}
being finite as a
\Gamma^{+}
-set for all
t
.

We claim that
\Sigma^{+}_{t}
is finite as a
\Gamma^{+}
-set if and only if it has a smallest element
\sigma_{t}
. The argument for this is similar to the one for the claim that an ideal in a valuation ring is finitely generated if and only if it is principal and will be omitted.

Assume
1
, and let
\sigma_{t}\in\Sigma^{+}_{t}
be smallest elements as above. Suppose that
\Sigma\neq\Gamma
. Let
\sigma=\sigma_{t_{0}}
be the smallest non-zero element among the
\sigma_{t}
, or equivalently the smallest element of
\Sigma^{+}\setminus\Gamma^{+}\neq\varnothing
. Then
[0,\sigma)\subseteq\Sigma^{+}_{0}=\Gamma^{+}
. Suppose that there exists a
g\in\Gamma
such that
0<g<\sigma
. Then
s=\sigma-g
satisfies
0<s<\sigma
, and moreover
s\in\Sigma\setminus\Gamma
since it is congruent to
\sigma
modulo
\Gamma
. This contradicts the minimality of
\sigma
. We conclude that the interval
(0,\sigma)
is empty, which means that
\sigma
is the smallest non-zero element of
\Sigma^{+}
, and hence
\Sigma
(and therefore
\Gamma
) is discrete.

Let
n\geq 1
be the smallest integer such that
n\cdot\sigma\in\Gamma
. Then
\gamma=n\cdot\sigma
is the smallest element of
\Gamma
. We claim that
\Sigma=\Gamma+{\mathds{Z}}\sigma
, which will imply the final assertion of
3
. The injective map
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma+{\mathds{Z}}\sigma\to\Sigma
satisfies the assumptions of the lemma and
1
. Since
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Sigma
have the same smallest positive element, namely
\sigma
, the argument in the previous paragraph shows that
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Sigma
.
∎

F. Kato’s theorem and its corollaries

For the results below, we recall the definition of the (saturated) affine blowup (or dilatation) of a monoid, see
[
Kat22
, §3]
. Let
P
be a saturated monoid,
K\subseteq P
an ideal, and
a\in K
an element. We denote by
P[K-a]
the saturation of the submonoid of
P^{\rm gp}
generated by
P
and
K-a
. It is fs if
P
is. The map

P\longrightarrow P[K-a]

is called an
affine blow-up
map. Note that the log schemes
{\mathds{A}}_{P[K-a]}
, for
a\in K
, form the standard affine cover of the (saturated) log blow-up
\Bl_{K}({\mathds{A}}_{P})
. This explains the name of the following result.

Lemma 2.5.5

(Valuative property of log blowups)
.

Let
\vartheta\colon P\to V
be a homomorphism of saturated monoids, with
V
valuative, and let
K\subseteq P
be a non-empty finitely generated ideal. Then there exists an
a\in K
and a factorisation

P\longrightarrow P[K-a]\longrightarrow V.

Proof.

Let
S\subseteq K
be a finite set generating
K
as an ideal. The map
\vartheta\colon P\to V
induces a linear preorder on
S
:
p\geq p^{\prime}
if
\vartheta(p)-\vartheta(p^{\prime})\in V
. Let
a\in S
be an element which is minimal with respect to this preorder. The map
P^{\rm gp}\to V^{\rm gp}
sends an element of the form
p-a
, for
p\in K=P+S
, to
\vartheta(p)-\vartheta(a)\in V
. This implies that it sends
P[K-a]
into
V
.
∎

Theorem 2.5.6

(F. Kato
[
Kat22
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

Let
P\to Q
be a homomorphism of fs monoids. Then there exists a non-empty ideal
K\subseteq P
such that for every
a\in K
, the induced map

P[K-a]\longrightarrow\left(Q\oplus_{P}P[K-a]\right)^{\rm sat}

is integral
2
2

2

Warning: the theorem involves saturated pushouts and integral morphisms. However, as we have already remarked in
Example

2.4.3
, integral morphisms are in general not stable under saturated pushout. Therefore taking a further saturated blowup may destroy integrality of the morphism.

(
Definition

defi:exactintsatvert monoids
2
).

In fact,
[
Kat22
, Theorem 1.1]
is the log scheme version of the above result. We will deduce
Theorem

2.5.6
from the proof of
[
Kat22
, Theorem 1.1]
. Given the relationship between integrality and flatness (see the proof below), we can regard
Theorem

2.5.6
as a monoid of analog of the Raynaud–Gruson “flattening by blow-up” theorem
[
RG71
]
.

Proof.

The proof of
[
Kat22
, Theorem 1.1]
applied to the map of log schemes
{\mathds{A}}_{Q}\to{\mathds{A}}_{P}
constructs a non-empty ideal
K\subseteq P
such that the morphism of schemes

{\mathds{A}}_{Q}\times_{{\mathds{A}}_{P}}^{\rm sat}\Bl_{K}{\mathds{A}}_{P}\longrightarrow\Bl_{K}{\mathds{A}}_{P}

is flat. This implies that for every
a\in K
, setting
P^{\prime}=P[K-a]
to be the affine blow-up monoid and
Q^{\prime}=(P^{\prime}\oplus_{P}Q)^{\rm sat}
, the ring homomorphism

{\mathds{Z}}[P^{\prime}]\longrightarrow{\mathds{Z}}[Q^{\prime}]

is flat. This implies that
P^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime}
is integral by
[
Ogu18
, Proposition I 4.6.7 (2)
\Rightarrow
(1)]
.
∎

Corollary 2.5.7
.

Let
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
be a homomorphism of fs monoids and let
P_{0}\to V
be a map into a valuative monoid
V
. Then there exists a factorisation
P_{0}\to P_{1}\to V
where
P_{0}\to P_{1}
is an affine blow-up
P_{0}[K-a]
for some non-empty ideal
K\subseteq P_{0}
and element
a\in K
such that the saturated base change
P_{1}\to Q_{1}=(P_{1}\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0})^{\rm sat}
is an integral homomorphism.

Proof.

Combine
Theorem

2.5.6
with
Lemma

2.5.5
.
∎

Proposition 2.5.8
.

Let
P
be a saturated monoid. The following are equivalent:

(1)

P
is sft over a valuative submonoid
V\subseteq P
,

(2)

P
is sfp over a valuative submonoid
V\subseteq P
,

(3)

P
is of type
(\mathrm{V})
,

(4)

there is a valuative monoid
V^{\prime}
and an sft morphism
V^{\prime}\to P
.

In fact, the monoid
V
can be taken to be the image of the morphism
V^{\prime}\to P
that is explicit in
4
and implicit in
3
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.2.10

4

\Leftrightarrow

1

we have that
1
\Leftrightarrow
2
, and trivially
2

\Rightarrow

3

\Rightarrow

4
. To show that
4
implies
1
let
V
be the image of
V^{\prime}
in
P
.
It is easy to check that
V
is valuative. Let
S\subseteq P
be a finite sat-generating set of
P
over
V^{\prime}
. Then
S
is also a finite sat-generating set for
P
over
V
. This shows that
V\to P
is sft.
∎

Corollary 2.5.9

(Analogue of Nagata’s theorem)
.

Let
V
be a valuative monoid and let
V\to P
be an injective homomorphism into a saturated monoid
P
which is finitely generated over
V
. Then
P
is finitely presented over
V
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.2.10
the morphism
V\to P
is sfp. We may write
V=\varinjlim V_{i}
as a filtered colimit of fs monoids.
Thanks to
Corollary

2.2.15
we may assume to have a compatible colimit
P=\varinjlim P_{i}
such that
V_{i}\to P_{i}
is sfp and
P_{j}\simeq(P_{i}\oplus_{V_{i}}V_{j})^{\rm sat}
for
j\geq i
. This means
P_{i}
is fs and the map
V_{i}\to P_{i}
is finitely presented. By
Corollary

2.5.7
, for large enough
i
, the morphism
V_{i}\to P_{i}
is integral. But then
P^{\prime}=V\oplus_{V_{i}}P_{i}
is integral, so that
P^{\prime}\to P
is injective. Moreover,
P^{\prime}=V\oplus_{V_{j}}P_{j}
for all
j\geq i
.

Let
S\subseteq P
be a finite set generating
P
as a monoid over
V
. As
P=\varinjlim P_{j}
, the set
S
is contained in the image of
P_{j}\to P^{\prime}
for large enough
j
. In this case the map
P^{\prime}\to P
is an isomorphism, showing that
P
is finitely presented over
V
.
∎

Remark 2.5.10
.

Corollary

2.5.9
is a monoid version of the result of Nagata (see
[
Nag66
]
,
[
SP
,
Lemma 053E
]
): a finitely generated and flat (equivalently, torsion free) algebra over a valuation ring is automatically finitely presented.

It is plausible that using
[
ALPT19
, Lemmas 2.1.7 and 2.1.9]
one could show an integral version of
Corollary

2.5.9
, i.e. that if
V
is valuative and
V\to P
is an injective homomorphism into an integral monoid
P
which is finitely generated over
V
, then
P
is finitely presented over
V
. However, it seems that this would require a version of
Theorem

2.5.6
employing un-saturated log blow-ups.

A proof of the (largely folklore) result below, a monoid analogue of Néron desingularisation, can be found in
[
GR03
, Theorem 6.1.31]
. It is not used later on but recorded here to shed some light on valuative monoids. We present a shorter proof than the one in
[
GR03
]
, based on toric resolution of singularities
3
3

3

We thank Jarek Buczyński for help with the proof of this proposition.
. Let us call a finitely generated monoid
P

quasi-free
if it is isomorphic to
{\mathds{N}}^{r}\times G
where
r\geq 0
and where
G
is an abelian group. This is equivalent to
\Spec({\mathds{C}}[P])
being smooth over
{\mathds{C}}
(see e.g.
[
Ful93
, §2.1, Proposition on p. 29]
).

Proposition 2.5.11
.

Let
V
be a valuative monoid. Then
V
is the increasing union of its quasi-free submonoids. In particular, if
V
is sharp, then
V
is the increasing union of its free submonoids.

Proof.

Let
P\subseteq V
be a finitely generated submonoid. We shall find a quasi-free submonoid
Q\subseteq V
containing
P
.

To this end, we may replace
P
with its saturation and
V
with
V\cap P^{\rm gp}
and hence assume that
P
is fs and
V^{\rm gp}=P^{\rm gp}
. Now, if
Q^{\prime}
is a free submonoid of
\overline{V}
containing the image
\overline{P}
of
P
, then its preimage
Q\subseteq V
is a quasi-free submonoid containing
P
. We may therefore assume that
V
(and hence also
P
) is sharp.

By toric resolution of singularities
4
4

4

The original reference
[
KKMSD73
, Theorem 11]
would work too, except that it does not assert the constructed resolution is a blow-up, so that we cannot apply
Lemma

2.5.5
but would need a version of it for a “proper” morphism of fans.

[
Niz06
]
there exists a non-empty ideal

K=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\subseteq P

such that the blow-up of
\Spec({\mathds{C}}[P])
along
V(K)
is smooth, and hence the union of
\Spec({\mathds{C}}[Q_{i}])
for a finite number of quasi-free monoids
Q_{i}=P[K-a_{i}]
with
P\subseteq Q_{i}\subseteq P^{\rm gp}
. By
Lemma

2.5.5
, this implies that there exists an element
a\in K
such that
Q=P[K-a]
is quasi-free and a factorisation

P\longrightarrow Q\longrightarrow V,

and since
Q^{\rm gp}=P^{\rm gp}=V^{\rm gp}
, the map
Q\to V
is injective.
∎

Tsuji’s theorem and its corollaries

To state Tsuji’s result we need some terminology. Let
P
be an fs monoid and
{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq P
a prime ideal of height one, with
F=P\setminus{\mathfrak{p}}
the corresponding face. Then
P/F={\mathds{N}}
(this is a canonical isomorphism as
{\mathds{N}}
has no automorphisms), and the induced function
v_{\mathfrak{p}}\colon P\to{\mathds{N}}
is the
valuation along
{\mathfrak{p}}
. Given a map of fs monoids
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
and a prime
{\mathfrak{q}}\subseteq Q
of height one whose preimage
{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq P
is also of height one, there exists a unique integer
e_{\mathfrak{q}}\geq 1
such that
v_{\mathfrak{q}}\circ\vartheta=e_{\mathfrak{q}}\cdot v_{\mathfrak{p}}
, called the
ramification index
of
\vartheta
at
{\mathfrak{q}}
.

We first show that étale maps are “tamely ramified” in prime ideals of height
1
, i.e. the respective ramification index is prime to
p
.

Lemma 2.5.12
.

The ramification indices of an étale map
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
are prime to
p
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{q}}\subseteq Q
be a prime ideal of height one such that
{\mathfrak{p}}=\vartheta^{-1}({\mathfrak{q}})
is also of height one. From the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\rm gp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\rm gp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{Z}}/e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}{\mathds{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta^{\rm gp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}

we deduce that the finite group
Q^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}
of order prime to
p
surjects onto
{\mathds{Z}}/e_{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathds{Z}}
and hence
e_{\mathfrak{q}}
is prime to
p
as well.
∎

Remark 2.5.13
.

The assertion of
Lemma

2.5.12
is false for smooth maps. For a simple example, let
P={\mathds{N}}\cdot e_{1}\subseteq Q=\langle e_{1},e_{2},-e_{1}+2e_{2}\rangle\subseteq{\mathds{Z}}^{2}
. Then
P\to Q
is smooth for any
p
, but its ramification index along
{\mathfrak{p}}=(e_{1},e_{2})
is
2
.

Theorem 2.5.14

(T. Tsuji
[
Tsu19
, I 5.4]
)
.

Let
P_{1}\to Q_{1}
be an integral homomorphism of fs monoids and let
n\geq 1
be an integer divisible by all of the ramification indices of
P_{1}\to Q_{1}
. Let
P_{2}=P_{1}
and let
P_{1}\to P_{2}
be the multiplication by
n
map. Then, the saturated base change
P_{2}\to Q_{2}=(P_{2}\oplus_{P_{1}}Q_{1})^{\rm sat}
is a saturated homomorphism.

Corollary 2.5.15
.

Let
P_{1}\to Q_{1}
be an integral homomorphism of fs monoids whose ramification indices are prime to
p
. Then there exists a Kummer étale map
P_{1}\to P_{2}
such that the saturated base change
P_{2}\to Q_{2}=(P_{2}\oplus_{P_{1}}Q_{1})^{\rm sat}
is a saturated homomorphism.

Remark 2.5.16
.

For
p=1
(i.e.
\Sigma=\varnothing
),
Theorem

2.5.14
and
Corollary

2.5.7
together imply that one can make a map of monoids
