P_{0}\to Q_{0}
saturated by an “alteration” (composition of a Kummer étale map and an affine blow-up), performed “locally” with respect to a given map
P_{0}\to V
into a valuative monoid
V
. The following diagram illustrates both theorems.

(2.5.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle V
divisible
saturated
\scriptstyle V
valuative
Kummer étale
integral
affine blowup

Here, the squares are pushouts in the category of fs monoids. The map
P_{1}\to V
exists (for a suitable choice of
P_{1}
) by
Lemma

2.5.5
since
V
is valuative. The map
P_{2}\to V
exists if
V
is furthermore divisible, as then the Kummer étale map
V\to(V\oplus_{P_{1}}P_{2})^{\rm sat}
admits a section.

Indeed, if
P
is a saturated monoid with
P^{\rm gp}
divisible, and
P\to Q
is a Kummer étale map, then we can write
Q^{\rm gp}=P^{\rm gp}\oplus M
with
M
a finite group, and then
Q=P\oplus M
(see
Lemma

2.6.1
), so
P\to Q
admits a section.

Corollary 2.5.17

(“Reduced fibre theorem”)
.

Let
V
be a valuative monoid and let
\vartheta\colon V\to P
be an sfp map. Suppose that either
p=1
or
V\to P
is étale. Then there exists a Kummer étale map
V\to W
such that the saturated base change
V\to Q=(W\oplus_{V}P)^{\rm sat}
is a saturated morphism of finite presentation.

Proof.

There exists a map of fs monoids
V_{0}\to P_{0}
and a map
V_{0}\to V
such that
P\simeq(V\oplus_{V_{0}}P_{0})^{\rm sat}
. If
V\to P
is étale, then we may further assume by
Proposition

2.4.8
that
V_{0}\to P_{0}
is also étale.

Next we use
Corollary

2.5.7
as in

2.5.1

to find an affine blow up
V_{0}\to V_{1}
and a factorization
V_{0}\to V_{1}\to V
such that the saturated base change
V_{1}\to P_{1}=(V_{1}\oplus_{V_{0}}P_{0})^{\rm sat}
is integral and
P\simeq(V\oplus_{V_{1}}P_{1})^{\rm sat}
. By assumption either
p=1
or
V_{1}\to P_{1}
is étale. Thus all ramification indices of
V_{1}\to P_{1}
are prime to
p
, either trivially or by
Lemma

2.5.12
.
Then
Corollary

2.5.15
applies to
V_{1}\to P_{1}
providing a Kummer étale
V_{1}\to W_{1}

such that
W_{1}\to Q_{1}=(W_{1}\oplus_{V_{1}}P_{1})^{\rm sat}
is a saturated morphism (still of fs monoids and thus of finite presentation by
Proposition

2.2.9
). Let
W=(V\oplus_{V_{1}}W_{1})^{\rm sat}
, so that
V\to W
is Kummer étale. Then

Q=(W\oplus_{V}P)^{\rm sat}=(W\oplus_{V_{1}}P_{1})^{\rm sat}=(W\oplus_{W_{1}}Q_{1})^{\rm sat}=W\oplus_{W_{1}}Q_{1}.\qed

The result below is an analogue of the following theorem of Grauert and Remmert
[
BGR84
, Corollary 6.4.1/5]
: if
A
is a reduced affinoid algebra over an algebraically closed non-archimedean field
K
, then the subring of powerbounded elements
A^{\circ}\subseteq A
is topologically finitely presented over the valuation ring
K^{+}=K^{\circ}
.

Corollary 2.5.18

(“Grauert–Remmert finiteness theorem”)
.

Let
V
be a divisible and valuative monoid and let
V\to P
be an sfp map. Then
V\to P
is saturated and
P
is finitely presented over
V
.

Proof.

Temporarily take
p=1
and apply
Corollary

2.5.17
, then use the fact that since
V^{\rm gp}
is divisible, every Kummer étale map
V\to W
admits a section. Indeed, since
V^{\rm gp}
is an injective object and
V^{\rm gp}\to W^{\rm gp}
is injective (
Lemma

2.1.1
), we may write
W^{\rm gp}=V^{\rm gp}\oplus(W^{\rm gp}/V^{\rm gp})
, and then we have
W=V\oplus(W^{\rm gp}/V^{\rm gp})
.
∎

With an argument similar to
Proposition

2.5.8
, we deduce:

Proposition 2.5.19
.

Let
P
be a saturated monoid. The following are equivalent:

(1)

P
is of type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
,

(2)

P
is finitely presented over a divisible valuative submonoid,

(3)

P
is finitely generated over a divisible valuative submonoid.

Lemma 2.5.20
.

Let
P
be a saturated monoid and let
F\subseteq P
be a face. If
P
is of type
(\mathrm{V})
(resp. of type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
), then so are
F
,
P_{F}
, and
P/F
.

Proof.

We will make use of the criterion of
Proposition

2.5.8
(resp.
Proposition

2.5.19
). Fix an sfp injective map
\vartheta\colon V\to P
with
V
valuative (resp. valuative and divisible) and let
S\subseteq P
be a finite sat-generating set.

We treat
F
first. By
Lemma

2.3.2
we see that
F
is sft over the valuative (resp. valuative and divisible) submonoid
W=F\cap\vartheta(V)
. By
Proposition

2.5.8
we deduce that
W\to F
is sfp.

For
P_{F}
, consider the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}}

where
G\subseteq V
is the preimage of
F
. Then
V_{G}
is valuative (resp. valuative and divisible) and
V_{G}\to P_{F}
is injective by
Lemma

2.1.1
. We claim that this map is sft, with a sat-generating set
T=S\cup-(S\cap F)
. Indeed, an element of
P_{F}
is of the form
p-f
(
p\in P
,
f\in F
), and we can write
np=v+\sum m(s)s
and
n^{\prime}f=v^{\prime}+\sum m^{\prime}(s)s
for
v,v^{\prime}\in V
and
m,m^{\prime}\colon S\to{\mathds{N}}
(cf. the proof of
Lemma

2.3.2
). The second equation shows that
v^{\prime}\in G
and
m^{\prime}(s)=0
for
s\notin F
. This implies that
nn^{\prime}(p-f)
belongs to the submonoid of
P_{F}
generated by
V_{G}
and
T
, and hence
T
is a sat-generating set.

For
P/F
, we consider the further square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V/G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P/F,}

Again,
V/G
is valuative (resp. valuative and divisible) and
V/G\to P/F
is injective. Moreover, the image of
S
(or
T
) in
P/F
is easily seen to be a sat-generating set. This finishes the proof.
∎

2.6.
More on Kummer étale maps

We gather additional facts about Kummer étale maps of saturated monoids, to be used in
Section

4
. Recall that for the definition of a Kummer étale map (
Definition

2.4.4
) we have fixed an implicit finite set of primes
\Sigma
and set
p=\prod_{\ell\in\Sigma}\ell
.

Lemma 2.6.1
.

Let
u\colon P\to Q
be a map of saturated monoids. The following are equivalent:

(1)

u
is Kummer étale (
Definition

2.4.4
),

(2)

u
is injective, the quotient
Q^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}
is finite of order prime to
p
and
P=Q\cap P^{\rm gp}
, i.e.
u
is exact (equivalently, if
Q
equals the saturation of
P
in
Q^{\rm gp}
).

Proof.

The only non-trivial statement is that a map satisfying (b) is sfp. Since
u
is injective, by
Proposition

2.2.10
, this is equivalent to sft. But
Q
is sat-generated over
P
by any subset
S
of
Q
whose image generates
Q^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}
: indeed, if
Q^{\prime}
is the submonoid of
Q
generated by
P
and
S
, then
(Q^{\prime})^{\rm gp}=Q^{\rm gp}
, and if
n
is the order of
Q^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp}
, then for every
q\in Q
we have
nq\in P^{\rm gp}\cap Q=P\subseteq Q^{\prime}
.
∎

If
P\to Q
is a homomorphism of monoids, we shall say that
Q
is
finite as a
P
-set
if there exists a finite set
S_{0}\subseteq S
such that
S=P+S_{0}
(cf.
[
Ogu18
, §I 1.2]
). As we have seen in
Example

2.5.3
, if
P\to Q
is a Kummer étale map between monoids of type
(\mathrm{V})
, it is not in general true that
Q
is finite as a
P
-set. In particular, in such cases the map
\Spec({\mathds{Z}}[Q])\to\Spec({\mathds{Z}}[P])
will be an integral morphism which is not of finite type. However, this issue disappears for monoids of type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
:

Lemma 2.6.2
.

Let
P\to Q
be a Kummer étale map between monoids of type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
. Then
Q
is finite as a
P
-set.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.5.19
,
P
is finitely generated over a divisible valuative submonoid
V
. If
n
denotes the exponent of
(Q^{\rm gp}/P^{\rm gp})
, we have inclusions
P\subseteq Q\subseteq\frac{1}{n}P
inside
P^{\rm gp}\otimes{\mathds{Q}}
. If
p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}
generate
P
over
V
, then
p^{\prime}_{i}=\frac{1}{n}p_{i}
generate
\frac{1}{n}P
over
\frac{1}{n}V
, which equals
V
because
V^{\rm gp}
is divisible and
V
is saturated. In particular,
p^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,p^{\prime}_{r}
generate
\frac{1}{n}P
over
P
. Let
S\subseteq\frac{1}{n}P
be the finite set of all sums
\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}p^{\prime}_{i}
with
n_{i}\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
. Then
S
generates
\frac{1}{n}P
as a
P
-set. We check that
S\cap Q
generates
Q
as a
P
-set: for
q\in Q
, we can write
q=p+s
for some
s\in S
and
p\in P
, and then
s=q-p\in S\cap Q^{\rm gp}=S\cap Q
.
∎

It is well-known that Kummer étale maps are not flat in general (consider e.g. the quotient map
{\mathds{A}}^{2}_{\mathds{Q}}\to{\mathds{A}}^{2}_{\mathds{Q}}/\mu_{2}
). For Kummer étale maps between valuative monoids, this does not occur.

Lemma 2.6.3
.

Let
V
be a valuative monoid and let
V\to W
be a Kummer étale map. Then
W
is valuative and is ind-free as a
V
-set. In particular, the homomorphism

{\mathds{Z}}[V]\longrightarrow{\mathds{Z}}[W]

is flat.

Proof.

Let
x\in W^{\rm gp}
, and let
n\geq 1
be such that
nx\in V^{\rm gp}
. Then either
nx
or
-nx
belongs to
V
and hence to
W
. Since
W
is saturated, one of
x
or
-x
belongs to
W
, showing that
W
is valuative.

For the second assertion, for
t\in W^{\rm gp}/V^{\rm gp}
, let
W_{t}\subseteq W
be the intersection of the corresponding coset with
W
. Then
W=\coprod_{t}W_{t}
as
V
-sets. Moreover, each
W_{t}
can be identified with a fractional ideal
J_{t}
of
V
(i.e., a sub-
V
-set of
V^{\rm gp})
: pick
w\in W_{t}
and consider the map
v\mapsto w+v\colon V^{\rm gp}\to W^{\rm gp}
. The preimage
J_{t}
of
W_{t}
maps bijectively onto
W_{t}
. We can write
J_{t}
as the inductive limit of its finitely generated sub-
V
-sets. Since
V
is valuative, every finitely generated fractional ideal is principal and free. Thus each
W_{t}
is and hence
W
is ind-free.
∎

Lemma 2.6.4
.

Let
P\to Q
be a Kummer étale map between saturated monoids. Then, the induced map
\Spec(Q)\to\Spec(P)
is a bijection.

Proof.

Let
F
be a face of
P
and let
G
be its saturation in
Q
. It is easy to check that
G
is a face of
Q
, and that this produces an inverse map
\Spec(P)\to\Spec(Q)
.
∎

The following lemma is well known for fs monoids. The argument works identically also for saturated monoids.

Lemma 2.6.5

(Vidal,
[
Ill02
, Lemme 3.3]
)
.

Let
u\colon P\to Q
be a morphism of saturated monoids such that the cokernel of
u^{\rm gp}\colon P^{\rm gp}\to Q^{\rm gp}
is torsion.
Then the map

\psi\colon(Q\oplus_{P}Q)^{{\rm sat}}\longrightarrow Q\oplus(Q^{{\rm gp}}/P^{{\rm gp}}),\qquad(a,b)\mapsto(a+b,b)

is an isomorphism.

Next, we discuss the “Kummer étale fundamental group” of a saturated monoid
P
. Recall that in
Definition

2.4.4
we fixed a set of primes
\Sigma
and denoted their formal product by
p
. Let

{\mathds{Z}}_{\Sigma}={\mathds{Z}}[{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}}\,:\,(n,p)=1]\subseteq{\mathds{Q}}.

By
Lemma

2.6.1
, there is an equivalence between the category of Kummer étale maps
P\to Q
and the category of group extensions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\rm gp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where
N
is finite of order prime to
p
. Let us call a monoid connected if its associated group is torsion free. If
P
is connected, then the category of connected Kummer étale
Q
over
P
is identified with the poset of subgroups
M\subseteq P^{\rm gp}\otimes_{\mathds{Z}}{\mathds{Z}}_{\Sigma}
containing
P^{\rm gp}
as a subgroup of finite index.

For the definition below, we pick an abelian group
\mu
isomorphic to
{\mathds{Z}}_{\Sigma}/{\mathds{Z}}
and denote by
\mu_{n}
or
{\mathds{Z}}/n{\mathds{Z}}(1)
its
n
-torsion subgroup. Later, we will mainly use
\Sigma=\{p\}
, and
\mu
will signify
\mu(k)=\varinjlim_{n}\mu_{n}(k)
for an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic exponent
p
. We denote by

\widehat{{\mathds{Z}}}^{\prime}(1)=\Hom({\mathds{Z}}_{\Sigma}/{\mathds{Z}},\mu)=\varprojlim_{(n,p)=1}{\mathds{Z}}/n{\mathds{Z}}(1)

its Tate module, which is a rank one free module over the prime-to-
p
(or prime-to-
\Sigma
) completion of
{\mathds{Z}}
.

Definition 2.6.6
.

Let
P
be a saturated monoid.
We define the
fundamental group
of
P
as

\pi_{1}(P)=\Hom(P^{\rm gp},\widehat{{\mathds{Z}}}^{\prime}(1)).

We note that there is a natural identification

\pi_{1}(P)=\Hom(P^{\rm gp},\Hom({\mathds{Z}}_{\Sigma}/{\mathds{Z}},\mu))=\Hom(P^{\rm gp}\otimes({\mathds{Z}}_{\Sigma}/{\mathds{Z}}),\mu)=\Hom((P_{\infty}^{\rm gp}\otimes{\mathds{Z}}_{\Sigma})/P^{\rm gp},\mu)

In particular,
\pi_{1}(P)
has the structure of a profinite group.

Lemma 2.6.7
.

Let
P
be a monoid of type
(\mathrm{V}_{\rm div})
. Then, the following hold.

(1)

The fundamental group
\pi_{1}(P)
is finitely generated (for arbitrary
p
).

(2)

For every
n\geq 1
, the group
P^{\rm gp}\otimes{\mathds{Z}}/n{\mathds{Z}}
is finite.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.5.19
we may choose an injection
V\hookrightarrow P
with
V
divisible valuative and
P
finitely generated over
V
. As
V^{\rm gp}
is divisible there is a non-canonical isomorphism
P^{\rm gp}\simeq V^{\rm gp}\oplus P^{\rm gp}/V^{\rm gp}
. The claims follow from
V^{\rm gp}/nV^{\rm gp}=0
for all
n
.
∎

3.
Log schemes beyond fs

In this section we develop logarithmic geometry beyond the case of fs log schemes. The most important notion is that of an sfp morphism (
Definition

3.3.1
) which is modelled on the notion of an sfp map of monoids from
Section

2.2
.

3.1.
Preliminaries on saturated quasi-coherent log schemes

A log scheme is
quasi-coherent
if it admits a chart étale locally
[
Kat89
, §2]
. For a monoid
P
we denote by
{\mathds{A}}_{P}
the scheme
\Spec({\mathds{Z}}[P])
endowed with the log structure associated to the natural map
P\to{\mathds{Z}}[P]
. By
[
Ogu18
, Proposition III 1.2.4]
, a chart
P\to{\mathcal{M}}_{X}(X)
on the log scheme
X
is the same as a strict map of log schemes
X\to{\mathds{A}}_{P}
.

If
f\colon X\to Y
is a strict map and
Y
is quasi-coherent, then
X
is quasi-coherent as well, simply by pulling back charts. Alternatively, this endows
\underline{X}
with the pullback log structure, which remains quasi-coherent.

A log scheme is
integral
(resp.
saturated
) if étale locally it admits a chart by an integral (resp. saturated) monoid. In particular integral (resp. saturated) log schemes are quasi-coherent by definition. The pullback log structure of an integral (resp. saturated) log structure is again integral (resp. saturated).

Remark 3.1.1
.

We warn the reader that our convention is slightly non-standard. In the literature (e.g. in
[
Ogu18
]
), integral (resp. saturated) log schemes are defined as log schemes
X
such that for every étale
U\to X
, the monoid
{\mathcal{M}}_{X}(U)
is integral (resp. saturated). It follows from
Lemma

3.1.2
below that a log scheme
X
is integral (resp. saturated) in our sense if and only if it is integral (resp. saturated) in the more standard sense and quasi-coherent. This discrepancy will not cause issues since all of the log schemes considered in this paper are quasi-coherent.

We spell out equivalent conditions for integral (resp. saturated) log schemes (well known for fs log schemes).

Lemma 3.1.2
.

Let
X
be a quasi-coherent log scheme.

(1)

The following are equivalent:

(a)

Étale locally on
X
, there exists a chart
P\to{\mathcal{M}}_{X}
with an integral (resp. saturated) monoid
P
, i.e.
X
is integral (resp. saturated).

(b)

For every étale
U\to X
, the monoid
{\mathcal{M}}_{X}(U)
is integral (resp. saturated).

(c)

For every geometric point
\overline{x}
, the monoid
{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{x}}
is integral (resp. saturated).

(d)

For every geometric point
\overline{x}
, the monoid
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{X,\overline{x}}
is integral (resp. saturated) and
{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{x}}
is
u
-integral (i.e.
{\mathcal{O}}_{X,\overline{x}}^{\times}
acts freely on
{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{x}}
).

(2)

Moreover, if
X
is integral (resp. saturated) and
P\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
is a chart with
P
arbitrary, then the induced map
P^{\rm int}\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
(resp.
P^{\rm sat}\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
) is a chart as well.

Proof.

1a

\Rightarrow

1d
: The stalk
{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{x}}
is the pushout of
P\leftarrow F_{\overline{x}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X,\overline{x}}^{\times}
where the face
F_{\overline{x}}
is the preimage of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X,\overline{x}}^{\times}
under
P\to{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{x}}
. Since
P
is integral and
F_{\overline{x}}\to P
injective, the pushout
{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{x}}
is
u
-integral. Moreover,
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{X,\overline{x}}
equals the quotient
P/F_{\overline{x}}
and thus is integral.

1d

\Leftrightarrow

1c
:
By
[
Ogu18
, Proposition I 1.3.3]
a monoid
Q
is integral if and only if
Q
is
u
-integral and
\overline{Q}=Q/Q^{\times}
is integral. The assertion about “saturated” then follows easily.

1c

\Leftrightarrow

1b
: This is part of
[
Ogu18
, Proposition II 1.1.3]
.

1b
and

1c
imply
1a
:
Let
P\to{\mathcal{M}}_{X}(U)
be a local chart, with
P
arbitrary. Since
{\mathcal{M}}_{X}(U)
is integral (resp. saturated) by
1b
, this map factors through
P^{\rm int}
(resp.
P^{\rm sat}
). We claim that the resulting map

P^{\rm int}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{X}(U)\qquad\text{(resp.\ $P^{\rm sat}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{X}(U)$)}

is another chart for
{\mathcal{M}}_{X}|_{U}
(which will also show (2)). Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{U}^{i}
(resp.
{\mathcal{M}}_{U}^{s}
) denote the log structure induced by
P^{\rm int}
(resp.
P^{\rm sat}
) on
U
. Then there are natural maps
{\mathcal{M}}_{X}|_{U}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{U}^{i}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{U}^{s}
,
which we show are isomorphisms by computing the stalks at a geometric point
\bar{u}
of
U
. Integralisation is a left adjoint, so commutes with pushouts. Therefore, and since
{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{u}}
is integral by assumption
1c
, we have

{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{u}}={\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{u}}^{\rm int}=\big(P\oplus_{\alpha^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X,\bar{u}}^{\times})}{\mathcal{O}}_{X,\bar{u}}^{\times}\big)^{\rm int}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}P^{\rm int}\oplus_{(\alpha^{\rm int})^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X,\bar{u}}^{\times})}{\mathcal{O}}_{X,\bar{u}}^{\times}={\mathcal{M}}_{U,\overline{u}}^{i}

where
\alpha\colon P\to{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{u}}
is the induced map.
Here we use that for monoids
A
,
B
and
C
we have
A^{\rm int}\oplus_{B^{\rm int}}C^{\rm int}=A^{\rm int}\oplus_{B}C^{\rm int}
, which is true since
B\to B^{\rm int}
is surjective. This shows the claim in the integral case.

Furthermore, saturation is a left adjoint, so commutes with pushouts. Therefore, since now
{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{u}}
is saturated by assumption
1c
, we have

{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\overline{u}}={\mathcal{M}}_{U,\overline{u}}^{{\rm sat}}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}({\mathcal{M}}_{U,\overline{u}}^{i})^{{\rm sat}}=\big(P^{\rm int}\oplus_{\alpha^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X,\bar{u}}^{\times})}{\mathcal{O}}_{X,\bar{u}}^{\times}\big)^{{\rm sat}}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}P^{\rm sat}\oplus_{\alpha^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X,\bar{u}}^{\times})}{\mathcal{O}}_{X,\bar{u}}^{\times}={\mathcal{M}}_{U,\overline{u}}^{s}.

Here we use that for integral monoids
A
,
B
and
C
we have
A^{\rm sat}\oplus_{B^{\rm sat}}C^{\rm sat}=A^{\rm sat}\oplus_{B}C^{\rm sat}
, which is true since
B\to B^{\rm sat}
is an epimorphism in the category of integral monoids. This shows the claim in the saturated case.

These arguments also show assertion (2).
∎

Remark 3.1.3

(Chart lifting property)
.

It is not clear whether every map between quasi-coherent log schemes locally admits a chart. Moreover, we do not expect the class of maps which admit local charts to be closed under composition, and we do not know if for maps
Y_{0}\to X
and
Y_{1}\to X
admitting local charts the fibre product
Y_{0}\times_{X}Y_{1}
is quasi-coherent. We will avoid such issues by considering sfp morphisms, see
Section

3.3
below, because such morphisms are examples for the following notion.

We say that a morphism
f\colon X\to S
of quasi-coherent log schemes has the
chart lifting property
with respect to a chart
\alpha\colon P\to{\mathcal{M}}_{S}(U)
on an étale open
U\to S

if the following holds. There is an étale covering
\{V_{i}\to U\times_{S}X\}
, monoid homomorphisms
\vartheta_{i}\colon P\to Q_{i}
, and commutative squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{A}}_{Q_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{A}}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

where the horizontal arrows are strict. So
U\times_{S}X
locally has a chart for
f
compatible with the given chart on
U
.
We say that a morphism
f\colon X\to S
of quasi-coherent log schemes has the
chart lifting property
if it has the chart lifting property with respect to every étale local chart of
S
.

By the final assertion
2
of
Lemma

3.1.2
, if
X
and
S
are integral (resp. saturated), then
X\to S
has the chart lifting property if and only if the above condition holds with
P
and
Q_{i}
integral (resp. saturated).

Moreover, one easily shows that the composition of two maps with the chart lifting property has the chart lifting property, and that the fibre product of two maps
Y\to S
and
X\to S
with the chart lifting property is quasi-coherent, with the two projections
X\times_{S}Y\to X
,
X\times_{S}Y\to Y
also with the chart lifting property. Moreover, since the saturation (resp. integralisation) map has the chart lifting property (essentially by construction, see
Remark

3.1.8
below), the last assertion holds for fibre products in integral (resp. saturated) log schemes. Furthermore, strict maps have the chart lifting property, of course.

Remark 3.1.4
.

It would be easy to construct charts for morphisms of log schemes if for affine log schemes
X
admitting local charts, the global sections
P={\mathcal{M}}(X)
would yield a chart
\id\colon P\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
.

Example

3.6.1
due to Takeshi Tsuji gives a counterexample against this optimistic constructions of charts.

The following lemma provides local charts for maps under some conditions.
Recall that an integral quasi-coherent log scheme
X
is
fine
if étale locally it admits a chart by a fine (finitely generated integral) monoid.

Lemma 3.1.5

(Beilinson
[
Bei13
, §1.1]
)
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
be a morphism from a quasi-coherent log scheme
X
to a log scheme
S
. Then
f
has the chart lifting property with respect to charts
P\to{\mathcal{M}}(S)
on
S
by finitely generated monoids
P
.

More precisely, if
X
is quasi-coherent (resp. integral, resp. saturated) and
\mu\colon P\to{\mathcal{M}}(S)
is a chart by a finitely generated (resp. fine, resp. fs) monoid
P
, then étale locally on
X
we can find a morphism
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
of monoids (resp. integral monoids, resp. saturated monoids) and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{A}}_{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{A}}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

where the horizontal maps are strict.

Proof.

[
Bei13
, §1.1]
asserts chart lifting for
f\colon X\to S
for fine charts on
S
and with
X
integral. The proof essentially carries over; we revisit Beilinson’s proof, which in turn is based on a proof by Kato.

Let
\bar{x}\to X
be a geometric point and replace
X
with a neighbourhood of
\bar{x}
such that
X
has a global chart
\nu_{0}\colon Q_{0}\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
. Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
be a finite set of generators of
P
. After replacing
X
with a neighbourhood of
\bar{x}
, we may assume that there exist
q_{i}\in Q_{0}
and units
u_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(X)
such that
f^{\ast}(\mu(p_{i}))=\nu_{0}(q_{i})\cdot u_{i}
in
{\mathcal{M}}(X)
. The map

\nu^{\prime}\colon Q^{\prime}=Q_{0}\oplus{\mathds{Z}}^{n}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}(X),\quad\nu^{\prime}(q,a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})=\nu_{0}(q)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}^{a_{i}}

is also a chart for
X
. We write
Q
for the image of
\nu^{\prime}
. The map
f^{\ast}\circ\mu
factors through a map
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
because this can be checked on a generating set for
P
.
The assertion now follows because by
Lemma

3.1.6
below, the inclusion
\nu\colon Q\to\Gamma(X,{\mathcal{M}}_{X})
is also a chart for
X
.

If
X
and
S
are integral (resp. saturated) and
P
is fine (resp. fs), then we may replace
\vartheta\colon P\to Q
as constructed above by the integralisation
P\to Q^{\rm int}
(resp. saturation
P=P^{\rm sat}\to Q^{\rm sat}
). The chart
Q\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
factors through a map
Q^{\rm sat}\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
by
Lemma

3.1.2
.
This is still a chart by the final assertion
2
of
Lemma

3.1.2
.
∎

The proof used the following simple lemma.

Lemma 3.1.6
.

Let
X
be a log scheme, let
Q^{\prime}
be a monoid, and let
\nu^{\prime}\colon Q^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
be a chart. Let
Q\subseteq{\mathcal{M}}(X)
be the image of this map. Then, the inclusion
\nu\colon Q\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
is a chart as well.

Proof.

The maps of prelog structures

\underline{Q}{}^{\prime}_{X}\twoheadrightarrow\underline{Q}{}_{X}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{X}

yield maps of associated log structures

(\underline{Q}^{\prime}{}_{X})^{\log}\longrightarrow(\underline{Q}{}_{X})^{\log}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{X}

whose composition is an isomorphism. Thus the first map is injective. Moreover, associated log structure is a left adjoint functor, so it preserves epimorphisms, and hence the first map is an epimorphism as well. But, in any category, a split monomorphism which is an epimorphism is an isomorphism. Therefore
(\underline{Q}{}_{X})^{\log}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{X}
is an isomorphism, and
\nu\colon Q\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
is a chart.
∎

On the existence of fibre products in quasi-coherent log schemes we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1.7
.

Let
f\colon X\to S
and
g\colon Y\to S
be maps of quasi-coherent log schemes. The fibre product
X\times_{S}Y
in the category of log schemes is quasi-coherent if one of the following holds.

(1)

One of the maps
f
or
g
is strict.

(2)

Étale locally on
X
,
Y
, and
S
there exists a chart

P\to{\mathcal{M}}(S)
and charts
P\to Q
and
P\to R
for
f
and
g
,
i.e.,
Q\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
and
R\to{\mathcal{M}}(Y)
are charts and the obvious maps commute.

(3)

The log schemes
X
and
Y
are integral and
S
is a fine log scheme.

Proof.

1
:
Let us assume
f
is strict. Then the projection
X\times_{S}Y\to Y
is also strict and charts for
Y
pull back to charts for
X\times_{S}Y
.

2
:
The fibre product can be constructed étale locally and the property of being quasi-coherent is étale local as well. So we may assume that both maps have charts with respect to a common chart of
S
as in the statement. Then the map

X\times_{S}Y\longrightarrow{\mathds{A}}_{Q}\times_{{\mathds{A}}_{P}}{\mathds{A}}_{R}={\mathds{A}}_{Q\oplus_{P}R}

is strict showing that
X\times_{S}Y
is quasi-coherent.

3
:
The assumptions on charts in
2
are met thanks to Beilinson’s
Lemma

3.1.5
.
∎

Remark 3.1.8
.

The inclusions of saturated log schemes into integral log schemes and further to quasi-coherent log schemes admit right adjoints. These are the
integralisation
of
X
denoted by
X^{\rm int}
and the
saturation
denoted by
X^{\rm sat}
.

The construction of saturation uses three steps (and works verbatim for integralisation). First, if
X\to Y
is strict and
Y^{\rm sat}
exists, then
X^{\rm sat}=X\times_{Y}Y^{\rm sat}
with the fibre product as log schemes and the projection
X^{\rm sat}\to Y^{\rm sat}
is strict. It therefore suffices to construct the saturation étale locally on
X
, because the local saturations will then automatically form a descent datum and glue to a saturation globally (gluing on the level of the underlying schemes is permissible since the morphisms to
X
are affine). We may thus assume that we have a global chart, and in fact that
X={\mathds{A}}_{P}
with the usual log structure induced by
P
. In this case
X^{\rm sat}
is simply
{\mathds{A}}_{P^{\rm sat}}
.

We note here that the maps
X^{\rm sat}\to X^{\rm int}
and
X^{\rm int}\to X
as well as their composition have the chart lifting property, essentially by construction.

We can now state three cases where saturated fibre products exist for saturated log schemes.

Lemma 3.1.9
.

In the category of integral (resp. saturated) log schemes the fibre product, denoted by

X\times_{S}^{\rm int}Y
(resp.
X\times_{S}^{{\rm sat}}Y
), exists if one of the following holds:

(1)

One of the maps
f\colon X\to S
or
g\colon Y\to S
is strict: then the fibre product
X\times_{S}Y
as log schemes is already integral (resp. saturated).

(2)

Étale locally on
X
,
Y
, and
S
there exist charts as in

Lemma

lem:existence of qcoh fibre products part 1
2
.

(3)

The log scheme
S
is a fine (resp. fs) log scheme.

In all these cases the integral (resp. saturated) fibre product has the form

\displaystyle X\times_{S}^{\rm int}Y

\displaystyle=(X\times_{S}Y)^{\rm int},

\displaystyle X\times_{S}^{{\rm sat}}Y

\displaystyle=(X\times_{S}Y)^{{\rm sat}}.

Proof.

Lemma

3.1.7
shows that in each of the cases
X\times_{S}Y
is quasi-coherent.
∎

We next prove some scheme-theoretic properties of integralisation and saturation.

Proposition 3.1.10
.

Let
X
be a quasi-coherent log scheme.

(1)

The morphism of schemes underlying the integralisation map
X^{\rm int}\to X
is a closed immersion.

(2)

The morphism of schemes underlying the saturation map
X^{\rm sat}\to X
is integral.
5
5

5

It is a bit unfortunate that the term “integral” appears in this article in several entirely different meanings: integral scheme, integral morphism of schemes, integral monoid/log scheme, integral morphism of monoids/log schemes. Although the intended meaning should always be clear from context, we nevertheless advise the reader to pay close attention.

(3)

If moreover
X
is an integral log scheme, then
\nu
is surjective.

Proof.

The scheme-theoretic properties in these assertions are all stable under base change and étale local on the target. By construction of integralisation and saturation, it is therefore enough to consider
X={\mathds{A}}_{P}
for a monoid
P
, i.e. the morphisms

{\mathds{A}}_{P^{\rm sat}}\longrightarrow{\mathds{A}}_{P^{\rm int}}\longrightarrow{\mathds{A}}_{P}

Since for every monoid
P
, the map
P\to P^{\rm int}
is surjective, the map
{\mathds{A}}_{P^{\rm int}}\to{\mathds{A}}_{P}
is a closed immersion, showing
1
. For
2
, we note that for every
q\in P^{\rm sat}
we have
nq=\iota(p)
for some
p\in P
and some
n\in{\mathds{N}}
, where
\iota\colon P\to P^{\rm sat}
is the canonical map. This means that the corresponding element
q
of
{\mathds{Z}}[P^{\rm sat}]
satisfies the integral equation
T^{n}=\iota(p)
.

To show
3
, it is enough to prove that every homomorphism
\vartheta\colon P\to k
to an algebraically closed field extends to
P^{\rm sat}
. The face
F=\vartheta^{-1}(k^{\times})
of
P
has a unique extension to a face
F^{\prime}\subseteq P^{\rm sat}
, see
Remark

2.3.1
. Moreover, since
P
is integral, the map
F\hookrightarrow F^{\prime}
is injecitve, and hence by
Lemma

2.1.1
also
F^{\rm gp}\hookrightarrow(F^{\prime})^{\rm gp}
is injective. The map
F\to k^{\times}
extends uniquely to
F^{\rm gp}
. Since
k^{\times}
is divisible and hence an injective object in the category of abelian groups, there exists a
\vartheta^{\prime}\colon(F^{\prime})^{\rm gp}\to k^{\times}
with
\vartheta^{\prime}|_{F}=\vartheta|_{F}
. We then extend
\vartheta^{\prime}|_{F^{\prime}}
to
\vartheta^{\prime}\colon P^{\rm sat}\to k
by sending
P^{\rm sat}\setminus F^{\prime}
to zero.
∎

3.2.
Prelog rings

In this subsection we discuss prelog rings and the associated log schemes.

A
prelog ring
is a monoid homomorphism
P\to A
from a commutative monoid
P
(written additively) to the underlying multiplicative monoid of a commutative ring
A
(equivalently a ring homomorphism
{\mathds{Z}}[P]\to A
).
Prelog rings form a category in the obvious way. This category has filtered colimits and is generated under filtered colimits by its compact objects, which are precisely the prelog rings
P\to A
where
P
is finitely presented and
A
is of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
(use
Proposition

2.2.5
which implies that the compact monoids are the finitely presented ones).

A prelog ring
P\to A
is
saturated
if
P
is saturated. The inclusion of saturated prelog rings into prelog rings admits a left adjoint, which is given by

(P\to A)^{\rm sat}=\big(P^{\rm sat}\to A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[P^{\rm sat}]\big).

Indeed, given a map
(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
with
Q
saturated, we get a unique factorisation
P\to P^{\rm sat}\to Q
, and then a unique
A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[P^{\rm sat}]\to B
. The category of saturated prelog rings has small colimits, with pushouts given by

(P\to A)\otimes_{(P_{0}\to A_{0})}(Q_{0}\to B_{0})=((P\oplus_{P_{0}}Q_{0})^{\rm sat}\to A\otimes_{A_{0}}B_{0},

and is generated under filtered colimits by its compact objects which are precisely the
(P\to A)
with
P
fs and
A
of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
(see
Corollary

3.2.3
below).

Proposition 3.2.1
.

Let
(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
be a morphism of saturated prelog rings. Then
(Q\to B)
is a compact object in the category of saturated prelog rings over
(P\to A)
if and only if

(1)

the homomorphism of monoids
P\to Q
is sfp, and

(2)

the induced homomorphism of rings
A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q]\to B
is of finite presentation.

Definition 3.2.2
.

A morphism of saturated prelog rings
(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
is
sfp
if the equivalent conditions of
Proposition

3.2.1
are satisfied.

Proof of
Proposition

3.2.1
.

The proof is entirely straightforward; we include it for completeness. Suppose
1
and
2
hold, let
(M\to R)=\varinjlim(M_{i}\to R_{i})
be a filtered colimit of saturated prelog rings over
(P\to A)
and let
(Q\to B)\to(M\to R)
be a morphism over
(P\to A)
.

Since
P\to Q
is sfp by
1
,
Q
is a compact object in the category of saturated monoids over
P
(
Proposition

2.2.10
), and hence the morphism
Q\to M
factors as
Q\to M_{i_{0}}\to M
for some index
i_{0}
.
Then
(R_{i})_{i\geq i_{0}}
becomes a system of
A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q]
-algebras.

For any prelog ring
(M^{\prime}\to R^{\prime})
over
(P\to A)
, extending a given homomorphism
Q\to M^{\prime}
over
P
to a morphism of prelog rings
(Q\to B)\to(M^{\prime}\to R^{\prime})
over
(P\to A)
is the equivalent to finding a homomorphism
B\to R^{\prime}
of
A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q]
-algebras. By condition
2
,
B
is a compact object in the category of
A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q]
-algebras, and hence the
A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q]
-algebra homomorphism
B\to R
above factors through
B\to R_{i}
for some index
i\geq i_{0}
, providing a factorisation

(Q\to B)\longrightarrow(M_{i}\to R_{i})\longrightarrow(M\to R).

Thus
(Q\to B)
is compact.

For the converse direction we suppose that
(Q\to B)
is compact. To show
1
, let
Q\to M
be a morphism over
P
with
M=\varinjlim M_{i}
a filtered colimit of saturated monoids over
P
. Then
(M\to 0)=\varinjlim(M_{i}\to 0)
as saturated prelog rings over
(P\to A)
. Therefore the map

(Q\to B)\longrightarrow(M\to 0)

factors through
(M_{i}\to 0)
for some
i
, and then
Q\to M
factors as
Q\to M_{i}\to M
over
P
, so that
Q
is compact. By

Proposition

2.2.10
property
1
follows.

To show
2
, let
R=\varinjlim R_{i}
be a filtered colimit of
A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q]
-algebras, and let
B\to R
be an
A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q]
-algebra homomorphism. Then
(Q\to R)=\varinjlim(Q\to R_{i})
in saturated prelog rings over
(P\to A)
, and thus the map
(Q\to B)\to(Q\to R)
factors through
(Q\to R_{i})
for some
i
, so that
B\to R
factors through
R_{i}
.
∎

The following corollary is immediate.

Corollary 3.2.3
.

A saturated prelog ring
(P\to A)
is a compact object in the category of saturated prelog rings if and only if
P
is an fs monoid and
A
is of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
.

Corollary 3.2.4
.

The composition of sfp morphisms of prelog rings is again sfp.

Proof.

This is formal using the characterisation as compact objects.
∎

Proposition 3.2.5
.

Let
P\to A=\varinjlim_{i}(P_{i}\to A_{i})
for a filtered direct system of prelog rings, and let

(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
be an sfp morphism of prelog rings.

Then, for large enough
i
, there is an sfp morphism
(P_{i}\to A_{i})\to(Q_{i}\to B_{i})
and a cocartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q\to B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q_{i}\to B_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P\to A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(P_{i}\to A_{i})}

in saturated prelog rings.

Proof.

By
Proposition

2.2.10
there exists a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}

in the category of saturated monoids where
P^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime}
is a morphism of fs monoids. Since
P^{\prime}
is a compact object in saturated monoids by
Corollary

2.2.14
, the map
P^{\prime}\to P=\varinjlim P_{i}
factors as
P^{\prime}\to P_{i}
for
i
large enough. We fix an initial choice
i_{0}
and restrict the filtered system to all indices
i\geq i_{0}
. Thus the system
(P_{i})
restricts to a system of monoids over
P^{\prime}
. For all such
i
we set
Q_{i}=(P_{i}\oplus_{P^{\prime}}Q^{\prime})^{\rm sat}
so that
Q=\varinjlim_{i\geq i_{0}}Q_{i}
. The maps
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
are sfp by
Lemma

2.2.8

Proposition

2.2.9
.

By assumption, the map

\varinjlim_{i\geq i_{0}}A_{i}\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P_{i}]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q_{i}]=A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q]\to B

is of finite presentation. Hence, for again large enough
i
, the
A\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q]
-algebra
B
descends to an
A_{i}\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P_{i}]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q_{i}]
-algebra
B_{i}
of finite presentation. This exhibits the desired sfp morphism
(P_{i}\to A_{i})\to(Q_{i}\to B_{i})
of prelog rings.
∎

For a prelog ring
(P\to A)
, we define

\Spec(P\to A)

to be the scheme
X=\Spec(A)
endowed with the log structure induced by
P\to A=\Gamma(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})
. For example, we have
{\mathds{A}}_{P}=\Spec(P\to{\mathds{Z}}[P])
.
If
Y
is a log scheme, then
({\mathcal{M}}_{Y}(Y)\to{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(Y))
is a prelog ring. These constructions define functors between the category of prelog rings and the opposite of the category of log schemes.

Lemma 3.2.6
.

We have an adjunction

(3.2.1)

\Hom(Y,\Spec(P\to A))\simeq\Hom((P\to A),({\mathcal{M}}_{Y}(Y)\to{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(Y))).

Proof.

By
[
Ogu18
, Proposition III 1.2.9]
, we have

\Hom(Y,{\mathds{A}}_{P})=\Hom(P,{\mathcal{M}}_{Y}(Y)).

Moreover, for every strict map of log schemes
X^{\prime}\to X
we have

X^{\prime}\simeq X\times_{\underline{X}}\underline{X}^{\prime}.

Applying this to the map
\Spec(P\to A)\to{\mathds{A}}_{P}
we obtain

\displaystyle\Hom(Y,\Spec(P\to A))

\displaystyle=\Hom(Y,{\mathds{A}}_{P})\times_{\Hom(Y,\Spec({\mathds{Z}}[P]))}\Hom(Y,\Spec(A))

\displaystyle=\Hom(P,{\mathcal{M}}_{Y}(Y))\times_{\Hom(P,{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(Y))}\Hom(A,{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(Y))

\displaystyle=\Hom((P\to A),({\mathcal{M}}_{Y}(Y)\to{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}(Y))).\qed

In particular, if
(P\to A)=\varinjlim\,(P_{i}\to A_{i})
where
(P_{i}\to A_{i})
is a filtered colimit of prelog rings, then we have

\Spec(P\to A)=\varprojlim\Spec(P_{i}\to A_{i})

is a cofiltered limit. If we choose
P_{i}\to A_{i}
, as we always can, to be compact in prelog rings, then we get the claim of the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2.7
.

Let
X
be an affine saturated log scheme with a global chart. Then
X
is the cofiltered inverse limit
\varprojlim\Spec(P_{i}\to A_{i})
of affine fs log schemes of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
.

3.3.
Maps of saturated finite presentation

We work in the category of saturated log schemes.

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
f\colon Y\to X
be a morphism of saturated log schemes.

(1)

The map
f
is called
locally of finite presentation up to saturation
or
locally sfp
for short if étale locally on
X
and
Y
it is isomorphic to the spectrum of an sfp map
\vartheta\colon(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
of prelog rings.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(Q\to B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(P\to A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(2)

The map
f
is called
of finite presentation up to saturation
or
sfp
for short if it is locally sfp and qcqs.

(3)

We denote the category of sfp morphisms with target
X
by
\mathbf{Sfp}_{X}
.

Remarks 3.3.2
.

(1)

In terms of charts,

Definition

defi:sfp log map
1
concretely means that
f
is locally sfp if
étale locally on
X
and
Y
it admits a chart (the horizontal maps are strict)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{A}}_{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{A}}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}

such that
\vartheta:P\to Q
is an sfp monoid homomorphism and the natural map

Y\longrightarrow X\times_{{\mathds{A}}_{P}}{\mathds{A}}_{Q}

is (strict and) of finite presentation. Note that the saturated fibre product
X\times^{\rm sat}_{{\mathds{A}}_{P}}{\mathds{A}}_{Q}
exists by
Lemma

3.1.9
and agrees with
X\times_{{\mathds{A}}_{P}}{\mathds{A}}_{Q}
.

(2)

For an sfp morphism
(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
of prelog rings the induced map

\Spec(Q\to B)\longrightarrow\Spec(P\to A)

is an sfp morphism.

(3)

A strict morphism
Y\to X
is locally sfp if and only if it is locally of finite presentation as a morphism of schemes (this is not immediate; see
Lemma

3.4.6
).

(4)

If
X
is an fs log scheme, the sfp morphisms
Y\to X
coincide with the morphisms of finite presentation of fs log schemes. This follows at once from

Proposition

2.2.9
.

(5)

The notion of a locally sfp map is étale local on source and target. More precisely, if
f\colon Y\to X
is locally sfp and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{V}}

a commutative diagram (not necessarily cartesian) with horizontal maps strict and étale, then
f_{V}
is also locally sfp. And if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}

are commutative squares as above where
\{U_{i}\to X\}
and
\{V_{i}\to Y\}
form strict étale covers, and the maps
f_{i}
are locally sfp, then so is
f
.

(6)

The base change of a (locally) sfp morphism by a strict morphism is again (locally) sfp.

Proposition 3.3.3
.

Let
f\colon Y\to X
be a morphism of saturated quasi-coherent log schemes.
Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

The morphism
f
is locally sfp.

(2)

For every strict étale map
U=\Spec(P\to A)\to X
, the map
V=Y\times_{X}U\to U
locally on
V
is of the form
\Spec(Q\to B)\to\Spec(P\to A)
for an sfp map of prelog rings

(P\to A)\longrightarrow(Q\to B).

(3)

For every strict étale map
U=\Spec(P\to A)\to X
and every filtered colimit of saturated prelog rings
(P\to A)=\varinjlim(P_{i}\to A_{i})
the following holds.

The map
V=Y\times_{X}U\to U
locally on
V
is, for large enough
i
, the saturated base change of
\Spec(Q_{i}\to B_{i})\to\Spec(P_{i}\to A_{i})
for an sfp map
(P_{i}\to A_{i})\to(Q_{i}\to B_{i})
of saturated prelog rings.

Diagram for visual aid:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(Q_{i}\to B_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U=\Spec(P\to A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec(P_{i}\to A_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

(4)

Étale locally on
X
and
Y
the morphism
f
fits into a cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}

in the category of saturated log schemes where the right hand vertical map is a morphism of fs log schemes of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
.

(5)

Étale locally on
X
and
Y
the morphism
f
fits into a cartesian diagram as above where the right hand vertical map is a morphism of finite presentation of fs log schemes.

Proof.

We shall prove

1

\Rightarrow

5

\Rightarrow

2

\Rightarrow

1

and
2

\Rightarrow

3

\Rightarrow

4

\Rightarrow

5
.

1

\Rightarrow

5
:
We may work étale locally on
X
and
Y
and assume that
Y\to X
is given by an sfp morphism
(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
of prelog rings. Choose a presentation

(P\to A)=\varinjlim_{i}\,(P_{i}\to A_{i})

as a filtered colimit where the
P_{i}
are fs monoids. The proof now follows by applying
Proposition

3.2.5
because the monoid
Q_{i}
is fs for a prelog ring
(Q_{i}\to B_{i})
that is sfp over a prelog ring
(P_{i}\to A_{i})
with
P_{i}
an fs monoid, see

Proposition

2.2.9
.

5

\Rightarrow

2
:
If assertion
2
holds for all
U^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\Spec(P^{\prime}\to A^{\prime})\to U\to X
with an étale covering
\{U^{\prime}_{\alpha}\to U\}
induced by maps of prelog rings
(P\to A)\to(P^{\prime}_{\alpha}\to A^{\prime}_{\alpha})
, then it also holds for
U\to X
. We may therefore work étale locally on
X
and
Y
, assume that
X=U=\Spec(P\to A)
and that there is a cartesian diagram as in
5
. Moreover, by further localising, because maps between fs log schemes have the chart lifting property for fs charts, we may assume that
Y_{0}\to X_{0}
is the map associated to a map
(P_{0}\to A_{0})\to(Q_{0}\to B_{0})
of prelog rings with
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
being fs monoids.

We write
P=\varinjlim_{i}P_{i}
as a filtered colimit of fs monoids
P_{i}
, and we set
X_{i}=\Spec(P_{i}\to A)
. Then
X=\varprojlim_{i}X_{i}
and
{\mathcal{M}}(X)=\varinjlim_{i}{\mathcal{M}}_{X_{i}}(X_{i})
since we evaluate on an affine scheme
\Spec(A)
. Since
P_{0}
is a compact object in saturated monoids, the map
X\to X_{0}
given on log structures by
P_{0}\to{\mathcal{M}}(X)
factors, for
i
large enough, via a morphism
P_{0}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{X_{i}}(X_{i})
and leading to a factorisation
X_{i}\to X_{0}
. Here we use that
X\to X_{i}
is an isomorphism on underlying schemes.

We denote by
Y_{i}\to X_{i}
the saturated base change of
Y_{0}\to X_{0}
. Note that these are fs log schemes and the map
Y_{i}\to X_{i}
is of finite presentation.
We therefore have chart lifting for
Y_{i}\to X_{i}
and fs charts. Working locally on
Y_{i}
we may thus assume that
Y_{i}=\Spec(Q_{i}\to B)
for a map
(P_{i}\to A)\to(Q_{i}\to B)
of prelog rings. Then
Y
is the saturated base change of a diagram of affine charted log schemes with charted morphisms. Hence
Y
itself is of the form
\Spec(Q\to B)
and
Y\to X
comes from a map of prelog rings
(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
, where
P\to Q
is the saturated base change of
P_{i}\to Q_{i}
, hence is sfp, because
P_{i}
and
Q_{i}
are fs.

2

\Rightarrow

1
: This is obvious from the definition of locally sfp morphisms of saturated log schemes.

2

\Rightarrow

3
:
By
2
we may assume that the map
Y\to X
is induced by an sfp morphism
(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
of prelog rings. The claim in this affine charted case then was proven already in
Proposition

3.2.5
.

3

\Rightarrow

4
:
This follows since étale locally
X
is of the form
\Spec(P\to A)
, and
(P\to A)
can be written as the direct limit of prelog rings
(P_{i}\to A_{i})
with
P_{i}
fs and
A_{i}
of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
. In this case, the maps
\Spec(Q_{i}\to B_{i})\to\Spec(P_{i}\to A_{i})
are morphisms of fs log schemes of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
.

4

\Rightarrow

5
:
This is obvious since every morphism between schemes of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
is of finite presentation.
∎

In particular, characterization
2
of locally sfp maps implies:

Corollary 3.3.4
.

Every locally sfp map of saturated log schemes has the chart lifting property (
Remark

3.1.3
).

Proposition 3.3.5
.

Let
f\colon Y\to X
be a locally sfp morphism of saturated log schemes.

(1)

Let
h\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be a morphism from a saturated log scheme
X^{\prime}
(
h
is not necessarily locally charted). Then the saturated pullback
Y^{\prime}=X^{\prime}\times^{\rm sat}_{X}Y
exists and the morphism
Y^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is locally sfp.

(2)

Let
g\colon Z\to Y
be another locally sfp morphism of saturated log schemes. Then the composition
Z\to Y\to X
is locally sfp.

(3)

Let
g\colon Z\to Y
be a morphism from a saturated log scheme
Z
such that the composition
Z\to Y\to X
is locally sfp. Then
Z\to Y
is locally sfp.

Proof.

1
:
Working étale locally on
X
,
Y
and
X^{\prime}
, we may assume that there exists a commutative square as in

Proposition

prop:sfp morphisms
5
:
Y\to X
is the saturated base change of a map of finite presentation
Y_{0}\to X_{0}
of fs log schemes along
X\to X_{0}
. So
Y^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is the saturated base change of
Y_{0}\to X_{0}
along the composition
X^{\prime}\to X_{0}
which exists by

Lemma

lem:saturated fibre product
2
.
Being a saturated base change of
Y_{0}\to X_{0}
shows by
Proposition

3.3.3
that
Y^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
is locally sfp.

2
:
The assertion is local on
X
,
Y
and
Z
.
We may therefore assume that
Y\to X
is isomorphic to the spectrum of an sfp map
(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
of saturated prelog rings. By
Proposition

prop:sfp morphisms
2
, locally on
Z
the map
Z\to Y=\Spec(Q\to B)
is induced by an sfp morphism
(Q\to B)\to(R\to C)
. Thus the composition
Z\to X
is induced by the composition

(P\to A)\longrightarrow(Q\to B)\longrightarrow(R\to C)

which is again an sfp morphism of prelog rings, see
Corollary

3.2.4
.
This shows that
Z\to X
is locally sfp.

3
:
The map
g
factors as
Z\to Z\times^{\rm sat}_{X}Y\to Y
which is the composition of the graph map and the base change of
Z\to X
. The graph map is the base change of the relative diagonal
\Delta_{Y/X}\colon Y\to Y\times^{\rm sat}_{X}Y
. So by
1
and
2
the claim follows from the special case of
\Delta_{Y/X}
as a map between saturated log schemes that are locally sfp over
Y
.

To study the diagonal we may work étale locally on
Y
and
X
. By
Proposition

3.3.3
we may assume that
Y\to X
is the saturated base change of a map
Y_{0}\to X_{0}
of finite presentation between fs log schemes. Here the diagonal
\Delta_{0}\colon Y_{0}\to Y_{0}\times^{\rm sat}_{X_{0}}Y_{0}
is of finite presentation between fs log schemes and thus sfp. The diagonal
\Delta_{Y/X}
is the saturated base change of
\Delta_{0}
and thus by
1
also sfp.
∎

3.4.
Approximation of sfp maps

In this subsection, we provide approximation results for sfp morphisms, in the spirit of
[
EGAIV3
, §8]
. This is a delicate task, since sfp maps are in general not of finite presentation as maps of schemes, and because we use saturated base change, which does not always agree with the scheme-theoretic base change.

We first deal with the case of an affine charted base. Let
(P_{i}\to A_{i})_{i\in I}
be a directed system of prelog rings with
P_{i}
fs and
A_{i}
of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
and let
(P\to A)=\varinjlim(P_{i}\to A_{i})
. Set
X=\Spec(P\to A)
and
X_{i}=\Spec(P_{i}\to A_{i})
.

Recall that
\mathbf{Sfp}_{X}
denotes the category of sfp morphisms
Y\to X
. Note that, since each
X_{i}
is fs and of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
, the category
\mathbf{Sfp}_{X_{i}}
consists of fs log schemes
Y_{i}\to X_{i}
whose underlying morphism of schemes is of finite type. For
j\geq i
, saturated base change along
X_{j}\to X_{i}
induces a functor
\mathbf{Sfp}_{X_{i}}\to\mathbf{Sfp}_{X_{j}}
, turning
\{\mathbf{Sfp}_{X_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
into a directed system of categories.

Proposition 3.4.1
.

Saturated base change along
X\to X_{i}
induces an equivalence of categories

\varinjlim\mathbf{Sfp}_{X_{i}}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}\mathbf{Sfp}_{X}.

We start with some easy category theory. Consider a category
\mathcal{C}
admitting small colimits and a map of inductive systems
\{P_{i}\to Q_{i}\}
indexed by a directed set
I
. We say that such a map is
cocartesian
if for every
j>i
the natural square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{i}}

is cocartesian. Suppose
\{P_{i}\to Q_{i}\}
is cocartesian. If
P=\varinjlim P_{i}
and
Q=\varinjlim Q_{i}
then for every
i\in I
the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{i}}

is cocartesian as well. This means that for every morphism
P\to R
and every
i
we have

\Hom_{P}(Q,R)=\Hom_{P_{i}}(Q_{i},R).

Lemma 3.4.2
.

Let
I
be a directed set,
\mathcal{C}
a category with small colimits, and let
\{P_{i}\to Q_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a cocartesian map of inductive systems such that
Q_{0}
is a compact object of
\mathcal{C}_{/P_{0}}
for some
0\in I
. Let
\{P_{i}\to R_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an arbitrary map of inductive systems. Set
P=\varinjlim P_{i}
,
Q=\varinjlim Q_{i}
, and
R=\varinjlim R_{i}
. Then

\varinjlim\Hom_{P_{i}}(Q_{i},R_{i})\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}\Hom_{P}(Q,R).

Proof.

We compute

\Hom_{P}(Q,R)=\Hom_{P_{0}}(Q_{0},R)=\Hom_{P_{0}}(Q_{0},\varinjlim R_{i})

which, because
Q_{0}
is compact and
\{P_{i}\to Q_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is cocartesian, equals

\varinjlim\Hom_{P_{0}}(Q_{0},R_{i})=\varinjlim\Hom_{P_{i}}(Q_{i},R_{i}).\qed

Lemma

3.4.2
will be used to obtain fully faithfulness in the proof below.

Proof of
Proposition

3.4.1
.

We first prove establish fully faithfulness, and then essential surjectivity.

Proof of fully faithfulness.
Let
0\in I
be an index and let
Y_{0}
and
Z_{0}
be objects of
\mathbf{Sfp}_{X_{0}}
. We must show that

(3.4.1)

\varinjlim_{i\geq 0}\Hom_{X_{i}}(Z_{i},Y_{i})\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}\Hom_{X}(Z,Y),

where
Z_{i}
,
Y_{i}
(resp.
Z
,
Y
) are defined for all
i\geq 0
by saturated base change of
Y_{0}
and
Z_{0}
to
X_{i}
(resp. to
X
).

Let us call
Y_{0}

special
if it is of the form
Y_{0}=\Spec(Q_{0}\to B_{0})
where
(Q_{0}\to B_{0})
is a compact object in the category of saturated prelog rings over
(P_{0}\to A_{0})
. Recall from
Proposition

3.2.1
that this means that
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
is sfp and
A_{0}\otimes_{{\mathds{Z}}[P_{0}]}{\mathds{Z}}[Q_{0}]\to B_{0}
is of finite presentation, thus every sfp
Y_{0}\to X_{0}
is locally of this form.

Step 1:
3.4.1
holds assuming
Y_{0}
is special.

If
Y_{0}=\Spec(Q_{0}\to B_{0})
is special, then compactness of
(Q_{0}\to B_{0})
combined with
Lemma

3.4.2
and
Lemma

3.2.6
together imply that (in the cofinal subsystem
i\geq 0
)

\displaystyle\Hom_{X}(Z,Y)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{(P\to A)}\big((Q\to B),(\mathcal{M}_{Z}(Z)\to\mathcal{O}_{Z}(Z))\big)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\varinjlim(P_{i}\to A_{i})}\big(\varinjlim(Q_{i}\to B_{i}),\varinjlim(\mathcal{M}_{Z_{i}}(Z_{i})\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{i}}(Z_{i}))\big)

\displaystyle=\varinjlim\Hom_{(P_{i}\to A_{i})}\big((Q_{i}\to B_{i}),(\mathcal{M}_{Z_{i}}(Z_{i})\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{i}}(Z_{i}))\big)

\displaystyle=\varinjlim\Hom_{X_{i}}(Z_{i},Y_{i}).

Here, we used the fact that, since
Z
is qcqs, we have

(\mathcal{M}_{Z}(Z)\to\mathcal{O}_{Z}(Z))=\varinjlim\,(\mathcal{M}_{Z_{i}}(Z_{i})\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{i}}(Z_{i})),

(see
Lemma

3.4.3
below).

Step 2:
3.4.1
holds assuming
Y_{0}
is a disjoint union of special objects.

Next, suppose that
Y_{0}
is the disjoint union of a (finite) number of special objects
\{Y_{0}^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in A}
. We treat the index set
A
as a discrete space. Then

\Hom_{X_{i}}(Z_{i},Y_{i})=\coprod_{a\colon|Z_{i}|\to A}\prod_{\alpha}\Hom_{X_{i}}(a^{-1}(\alpha),Y^{\alpha}_{i}),

and the same with
\Hom_{X}(Z,Y)
. To pass to the limit observe that since
|Z|=\varprojlim|Z_{i}|
(inverse limit of spectral spaces with quasi-compact transition maps), every locally constant function
|Z|\to A
factors through one of the projections
\pi_{j}\colon|Z|\to|Z_{j}|
(see
[
SP
,
Lemma 0A2Y
]
). Therefore

\displaystyle\Hom_{X}(Z,Y)

\displaystyle=\coprod_{a\colon|Z|\to A}\prod_{\alpha}\Hom_{X}(a^{-1}(\alpha),Y^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{j}\coprod_{a\colon|Z_{j}|\to A}\prod_{\alpha}\Hom_{X}(\pi_{j}^{-1}a^{-1}(\alpha),Y^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{j}\coprod_{a\colon|Z_{j}|\to A}\prod_{\alpha}\varinjlim_{i\geq j}\Hom_{X_{i}}(\pi_{ij}^{-1}a^{-1}(\alpha),Y_{i}^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{i}\coprod_{a\colon|Z_{i}|\to A}\prod_{\alpha}\Hom_{X_{i}}(a^{-1}(\alpha),Y^{\alpha}_{i})

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{i}\Hom_{X_{i}}(Z_{i},Y_{i}).

Here, for
i\geq j
we denoted by
\pi_{ij}
the map
X_{i}\to X_{j}
. In the fourth equality, we commute the filtered colimit
\varinjlim_{i\geq j}
past the finite limit
\prod_{\alpha}
, then past the colimit
\coprod_{a}
, and finally
\varinjlim_{j}\varinjlim_{i\geq j}
is the same as
\varinjlim_{i}
.

Step 3:
3.4.1
holds in general.

Now, consider the general case. We pick a strict étale surjection
U_{0}\to Y_{0}
with
U_{0}
a finite disjoint union of special objects, and a strict étale surjection
U^{\prime}_{0}\to U_{0}\times_{Y_{0}}U_{0}
with
U^{\prime}_{0}
a finite disjoint union of special objects. Define
U_{i}=U_{0}\times_{Y_{0}}Y_{i}
and
U=U_{0}\times_{Y_{0}}Y
, and analogously with
U^{\prime}_{i}
and
U^{\prime}
. Then
Y_{i}
is the coequalizer of
U^{\prime}_{i}\rightrightarrows U_{i}
(in sheaves on the big etale site of
\underline{X}
) where the two maps are strict étale. Let
f\colon Z\to Y
be a morphism over
X
. We will show that there exists a morphism
f_{i}\colon Z_{i}\to Y_{i}
inducing
f
, and that any two such
f_{i}
become equal after base change to
X_{j}
for
j\gg i
. Let
V=Z\times_{Y}U
and
V^{\prime}=Z\times_{Y}U^{\prime}
. Then
Z
is the coequalizer of the two strict étale maps
V^{\prime}\rightrightarrows V
. Since
Z=\varprojlim Z_{i}
as schemes, we may assume that there exists a coequalizer diagram
V^{\prime}_{0}\rightrightarrows V_{0}\to Z_{0}
of strict étale maps whose base change is
V^{\prime}\rightrightarrows V\to Z
. Since
U_{0}
is the disjoint union of special objects, the result of the previous paragraph applied to
V\to U
produces an (essentially unique) map
g_{i}\colon V_{i}\to U_{i}
inducing
V\to U
. Similarly, we obtain a map
g^{\prime}_{i}\colon V^{\prime}_{i}\to U^{\prime}_{i}
. Diagrams for visual aid:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}?}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Looking at the four compositions in the left square

g_{i}s,\quad sg^{\prime}_{i},\quad g_{i}t,\quad tg^{\prime}_{i}\quad\colon\quad V^{\prime}_{i}\longrightarrow U_{i},

since
gs=sg^{\prime}
and
gt=tg^{\prime}
, we must have
g_{i}s=sg^{\prime}_{i}
and
g_{i}t=tg^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\gg 0
. We have thus obtained a natural transformation between the two
(\bullet\rightrightarrows\bullet)
-shaped diagrams, yielding a map
f_{i}\colon Z_{i}\to Y_{i}
between their coequalizers. It is clear from the construction that
f
is the base change of
f_{i}
to
X
. Moreover, retracing the argument, we see that any two solutions to this problem become equal for
i\gg 0
. This finishes the proof of fully faithfulness.

Proof of essential surjectivity.
Let
Y\to X
be an sfp map. If
Y
is special, i.e. of the form
\Spec(Q\to B)
for an sfp map
(P\to A)\to(Q\to B)
, then we have already established in

Proposition

3.2.5
that
Y
is in the essential image. Therefore finite disjoint unions of special objects are in the image as well. Moreover, if
U
is in the essential image and
V\to U
is a strict morphism of finite presentation, then
V
is in the essential image as well, simply by the corresponding fact for schemes
[
EGAIV3
, Théorème 8.5.2]
(and the fact that strict finitely presented maps are sfp).

With these preliminary observations, let
U\to Y
be a strict étale surjection with
U
a finite disjoint union of special objects, and let
R=U\times_{Y}U
. Then
U
is in the essential image and as the first projection
R\to U
is strict étale, so is
R
. Consider the corresponding étale groupoid
(U,R,s,t,c)
or rather
(U,R,s,t,c,e,i)
with étale maps
s,t\colon R\to U
(source and target),
c\colon R\times_{t,U,s}R\to R
(composition),
e\colon U\to R
(identity), and
i\colon R\to R
(inverse). All of these are étale morphisms over
U
, satisfying certain identities (see
[
SP
,
Section 0230
]
) taking place in maps between some fibre products of
R
over
U
. Let
U_{i}
and
R_{i}
be sfp over
X_{i}
with saturated base change
U
and
R
. By fully faithfulness, possibly after increasing
i
there exist morphisms
s_{i},t_{i}\colon R_{i}\to U_{i}
etc. (Note that since
R_{i}\to U_{i}
is strict étale,
R_{i}\times_{U_{i}}R_{i}
is sfp over
X_{i}
, with saturated base change to
X
isomorphic to
R\times_{U}R
.) Moreover, the axioms of a groupoid will be satisfied for
i\gg 0
.

Let
Y_{i}
be the algebraic stack over
X_{i}
defined by the étale groupoid
(U_{i},R_{i},\ldots)
. These form an inverse system with affine transition maps and inverse limit
Y
. Moreover,
Y
and all
Y_{i}
are qcqs. Since
Y
is a scheme, by
[
Ryd15
, Theorem C(iii)]
the
Y_{i}
are schemes for
i\gg 0
.

Coming back from our brief detour through groupoids and stacks, we establish that for
i\gg 0
the coequalizer
Y_{i}
of
R_{i}\rightrightarrows U_{i}
exists as a scheme. Moreover,
U_{i}\to Y_{i}
is an étale surjection and
R_{i}=U_{i}\times_{Y_{i}}U_{i}
. Since log structures form an étale stack, this enables us to construct the log structure on
Y_{i}
for which
U_{i}\to Y_{i}
is strict. Then, since the formation of étale coequalizers commutes with base change
[
SP
,
Lemma 03I4
]
, we deduce that
Y
is the saturated base change of
Y_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 3.4.3
.

Let
(X_{i})
be an inverse system of log schemes with affine transition maps and limit
X
. Suppose that
X_{i}
are qcqs. Then

\varinjlim{\mathcal{M}}(X_{i})\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}{\mathcal{M}}(X).

Proof.

We have
\underline{X}=\varprojlim\underline{X}_{i}
, and the same with underlying topological spaces. In particular,
\underline{X}
is qcqs. We claim that the map of étale sheaves on
\underline{X}

\varinjlim\pi_{i}^{-1}({\mathcal{M}}_{X_{i}})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{X}

is an isomorphism. To this end, first note that since
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{\times}=\varprojlim\pi_{i}^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{X_{i}}^{\times})
, the source (call it
{\mathcal{M}}
) of the map in question is a log structure. Moreover, the log scheme
(\underline{X},{\mathcal{M}})
has the universal property of the inverse limit
\varprojlim X_{i}
, and hence
{\mathcal{M}}\simeq{\mathcal{M}}_{X}
.

Since
X
is qcqs, in view of
[
SP
,
Lemma 09YN
]
(see also
[
SGA 42
, Exp. VI, §5]
) for any system of sheaves of sets
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
on
X_{i}
together with maps
\pi_{ij}^{-1}({\mathcal{F}}_{i})\to{\mathcal{F}}_{j}
for
j\geq i
, satisfying the natural transitivity property, we have

\varinjlim\Gamma(X_{i},{\mathcal{F}}_{i})=\Gamma(X,\varinjlim\pi^{-1}_{i}{\mathcal{F}}_{i}).

The result follows by taking
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}={\mathcal{M}}_{X_{i}}
.
∎

We can strengthen
Proposition

3.4.1
as follows.

Definition 3.4.4
.

We call an inverse system of saturated log schemes
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}

affine charted
if there exists a set
A
, direct systems of saturated prelog rings

\{(P_{\alpha,i}\to A_{\alpha,i})\}_{i\in I^{\rm op}}\quad\text{for $\alpha\in A$}

and strict étale maps forming a morphism of inverse systems

f_{\alpha,i}\colon U_{\alpha,i}=\Spec(P_{\alpha,i}\to A_{\alpha,i})\longrightarrow X_{i}

and such that for every index
i
, the family
\{f_{\alpha,i}\colon U_{\alpha,i}\to X_{i}\}_{\alpha\in A}
is a covering family for the étale topology on
\underline{X}_{i}
.

Note that in particular the transition maps
X_{j}\to X_{i}
in an affine charted inverse system are affine. Since affine morphisms are in particular qcqs, if
X_{i}
is qcqs for some index
i
, then
X_{j}
(
j\geq i
) and
X
are qcqs as well. The inverse limit
\varprojlim X_{i}
exists and is a saturated log scheme, and

U_{\alpha}=\Spec(P_{\alpha}\to A_{\alpha}),\quad(P_{\alpha}\to A_{\alpha})=\varinjlim_{i\in I^{\rm op}}\,(P_{\alpha,i}\to A_{\alpha,i})

forms a strict étale cover of
X
. Moreover, we have

{\mathcal{M}}_{X}=\varinjlim_{i\in I^{\rm op}}\pi^{-1}_{i}({\mathcal{M}}_{X_{i}})

where
\pi_{i}\colon X\to X_{i}
is the projection.

Our main approximation result is the following.

Theorem 3.4.5
.

Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an affine charted system of qcqs saturated log schemes with inverse limit
X
. Then, saturated base change induces an equivalence of categories

\varinjlim\mathbf{Sfp}_{X_{i}}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}\mathbf{Sfp}_{X}

Proof.

Since the underlying scheme of
X
is the inverse limit of the underlying schemes of
X_{i}
, if
U\to X
is an étale map with
U
qcqs, then it descends to
U_{i}\to X_{i}
. Then the association
U\mapsto\varinjlim\mathbf{Sfp}_{/U_{i}}
defines a stack on the category of qcqs (strict) étale maps
U\to X
. Similarly,
U\mapsto\mathbf{Sfp}_{/U}
is a stack, and our functor is the global sections of a morphism of stacks. This implies that we can work locally, and hence we may assume that
X=\Spec(P\to A)
is the inverse limit of
X_{i}=\Spec(P_{i}\to A_{i})
.

Now,
Proposition

3.4.1
cannot be applied yet since
P_{i}
might not be fs and
A_{i}
may not be of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
. However, let
J
be the poset of pairs
j=(i(j),P^{\prime}_{j}\to A^{\prime}_{j})
where
i=i(j)\in I
, where
P^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq P_{i(j)}
is a finitely generated (and hence fs) submonoid, and where
A^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq A_{i(j)}
is a finitely generated
{\mathds{Z}}
-algebra. We have
j^{\prime}=(i(j^{\prime}),P^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}\to A^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})\geq(i(j),P^{\prime}_{j}\to A^{\prime}_{j})=j
if
i(j^{\prime})\geq i(j)
,
P_{i(j)}\to P_{i(j^{\prime})}
sends
P^{\prime}_{j}
inside
P^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
, and
A_{i(j)}\to A_{i(j^{\prime})}
sends
A^{\prime}_{j}
inside
A^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
. Then the projection
\pi\colon J\to I
,
\pi(j)=i(j)
is monotone and cofinal, thus
X=\varprojlim_{J}X^{\prime}_{j}
where
X^{\prime}_{j}=\Spec(P^{\prime}_{j}\to A^{\prime}_{j})
. Moreover, for every
i\in I
we have
X_{i}=\varprojlim_{J_{i}}X^{\prime}_{j}
where
J_{i}=\pi^{-1}(i)\subseteq J
. We then have, by
Proposition

3.4.1
applied both to
X=\varinjlim_{J}X_{i}
and
X_{i}=\varinjlim_{J_{i}}X_{j}

\mathbf{Sfp}_{X}=\varinjlim_{J}\mathbf{Sfp}_{X^{\prime}_{j}}=\varinjlim_{I}\left(\varinjlim_{J_{i}}\mathbf{Sfp}_{X^{\prime}_{j}}\right)=\varinjlim_{I}\mathbf{Sfp}_{X_{i}}.\qed

The following innocent-looking result is surprisingly not so easy to prove.

Lemma 3.4.6
.

Let
f\colon Y\to X
be a strict map of saturated log schemes. Then
f
is (locally) sfp if and only if the underlying morphism of schemes
\underline{f}\colon\underline{Y}\to\underline{X}
is (locally) of finite presentation.

Proof.

The “if” direction is clear. For the converse, suppose that
f
is sfp. Working strict étale locally, see
[
SP
,
Lemma 05B0
]
, we may assume by
Proposition

3.3.3
that we are in the following situation: there is a directed system of prelog rings
(P_{i}\to A_{i})
with
P_{i}
fs and
A_{i}
of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
and direct limit
(P\to A)
, and sfp maps
(P_{i}\to A_{i})\to(Q_{i}\to B_{i})
such that

(Q_{j}\to B_{j})=(Q_{i}\to B_{i})\otimes^{\rm sat}_{(P_{i}\to A_{i})}(P_{j}\to B_{j})\qquad\text{for $j\geq i$}

(where
\otimes^{\rm sat}
denotes pushout in the category of saturated prelog rings), with direct limit
(Q\to B)
such that setting
X_{i}=\Spec(P_{i}\to A_{i})
and
Y_{i}=\Spec(Q_{i}\to B_{i})
we have

(Y\to X)\quad\simeq\quad(\Spec(Q\to B)\to\Spec(P\to A))\quad\simeq\quad\varprojlim(Y_{i}\to X_{i}).

Since
Y\to X
is strict, the map of prelog rings
(P\to B)\to(Q\to B)
induces an isomorphism

Y=\Spec(Q\to B)\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}\Spec(P\to B)=\varprojlim\Spec(P\to A\otimes_{A_{i}}B_{i})=\varprojlim Y^{\prime}_{i}.

of saturated log schemes over
X
, where
Y^{\prime}_{i}=\Spec(P\to A\otimes_{A_{i}}B_{i})
.
Now, since
(Q\to B)
is a compact object in the category of saturated prelog rings over
(P\to A)
, we have

\displaystyle\Hom_{X}(\varprojlim Y^{\prime}_{i},Y)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{(P\to A)}((Q\to B),\varinjlim({\mathcal{M}}(Y^{\prime}_{i})\to{\mathcal{O}}(Y^{\prime}_{i})))

\displaystyle=\varinjlim\Hom_{(P\to A)}((Q\to B),({\mathcal{M}}(Y^{\prime}_{i})\to{\mathcal{O}}(Y^{\prime}_{i})))

\displaystyle=\varinjlim\Hom_{X}(Y^{\prime}_{i},Y).

Therefore the inverse isomorphism
\varprojlim Y^{\prime}_{i}\xlongrightarrow{\,\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\displaystyle\sim$}}\,}Y
factors through some
Y^{\prime}_{i}
. This means that
Y
is a retract of
Y^{\prime}_{i}
for some
i
. Thus
\underline{Y}=\Spec(B)
is a retract of
\underline{Y}^{\prime}_{i}=\Spec(A\otimes_{A_{i}}B_{i})
, and since
A\to A\otimes_{A_{i}}B_{i}
is of finite presentation, so is
A\to B
.
∎

Corollary 3.4.7
.

Let
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an affine charted system of qcqs saturated log schemes with inverse limit
X
. Let
Z_{0}\to Y_{0}
be a morphism between sfp log schemes over
X_{0}
. Then, the saturated base change
Z\to Y
to
X
is strict if and only if the saturated base change
Z_{i}\to Y_{i}
to
X_{i}
is strict for
i\gg 0
.

Proof.

If
Z\to Y
is strict, then by
Lemma

3.4.6
the underlying map of schemes
\underline{Z}\to\underline{Y}
is of finite presentation. Since
\underline{Y}=\varinjlim\underline{Y}{}_{i}
(with affine transition maps
Y_{j}\to Y_{i}
), by
[
EGAIV3
, Théorème 8.5.2]
there exists a morphism of finite presentation
\underline{Z}{}^{\prime}_{i}\to\underline{Y}{}_{i}
for some index
i
whose base change to
\underline{Y}
is
\underline{Z}\to\underline{Y}
. Let
Z^{\prime}_{i}
be
\underline{Z}{}^{\prime}_{i}
with log structure making
Z{}^{\prime}_{i}\to\underline{Y}{}_{i}
strict. Then
Z^{\prime}_{i}\to Y_{i}
is sfp and its (saturated or not) base change to
X_{i}
is
Z\to Y
. Therefore (by the fully faithfulness part of
Theorem

3.4.5
), after increasing the index
i
, there exists an isomorphism
Z^{\prime}_{i}\simeq Z_{i}
over
Y_{i}
. Since
Z^{\prime}_{i}\to Y_{i}
is strict, so is
Z_{i}\to Y_{i}
. The other direction is clear.
∎

No absolute fs approximation

A nice complement to the noetherian approximation results of
[
EGAIV3
, §8]
is the absolute approximation theorem of Thomason–Trobaugh
[
TT90
, Theorem C.9]
: every qcqs scheme can be written as the inverse limit of schemes of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
with affine transition maps. Our relative approximation result for sfp maps (
Theorem

3.4.5
) as well as the fact that every affine charted saturated log scheme
\Spec(P\to A)
can be written as the inverse limit
\Spec(P_{i}\to A_{i})
where
P_{i}
are fs and
A_{i}
are of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
may suggest that a similar result to the Thomason–Trobaugh approximation theorem could hold for log schemes. For example, one could hope that every saturated qcqs log scheme can be written as the limit of an affine charted inverse system of fs log schemes of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
. Unfortunately, this is not true, as the following example shows.

Example 3.4.8
.

We start with any noetherian scheme
X
covered by two connected opens
U_{+},U_{-}
whose intersection
U=U_{+}\cap U_{-}
has two connected components
U_{0},U_{1}
. For the simplest example, let
k
be a connected noetherian ring in which
2
is invertible and let

X=\Spec(A),\qquad A=k[X,Y]/((Y-X^{2}+1)(Y+X^{2}-1))

be the union of two parabolas intersecting in two points
x_{\pm}=(\pm 1,0)
. Let

U_{\pm}=X\setminus\{x_{\pm}\}=\Spec(A[1/(X\mp 1)]

and let
U=U_{+}\cap U_{-}
, which is isomorphic to the disjoint union of two copies
U_{0}
,
U_{1}
of
{\mathds{A}}^{1}_{k}\setminus\{\pm 1\}
.

Consider the saturated log structure on
X
constructed in the following way. Let

P=\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathds{Z}}}{\mathds{N}}T^{n}

and let
T\colon P\to P
be the automorphism induced by
T^{n}\mapsto T^{n+1}
. We give
U_{\pm}
the log structure charted by the map
P\to{\mathcal{O}}(U_{\pm})
sending
P\setminus\{0\}
to zero. We glue these log structures on
U_{\pm}
to a log structure on
X
using the identity
P\to P
on
U_{0}
and the automorphism
T
on
U_{1}
.

Let us describe the monodromy of the locally constant sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}=\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm gp}_{X}
. The nerve of the cover
X=U_{+}\cup U_{-}
by two connected opens with intersection
U=U_{0}\sqcup U_{1}
with two connected components is homotopy equivalent to
S^{1}
and hence induces a surjection

\pi_{1}^{\rm SGA3}(X,\overline{x})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(S^{1})

where
\pi_{1}^{\rm SGA3}(X,\overline{x})
is
groupe fondamental élargi
introduced in
[
SGA3II
, Exp. X, §6]
(see also
[
BS15
, Lemma 7.4.3]
). It has the property that the category of locally constant étale sheaves of sets on
X
is equivalent to the category of sets endowed with a continuous action of
\pi_{1}^{\rm SGA3}(X,\overline{x})
. Since
{\mathcal{F}}
is constant on
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
, the monodromy action of
\pi_{1}^{\rm SGA3}(X,\overline{x})
on
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bar{x}}
factors through
\pi_{1}(S^{1})\simeq{\mathds{Z}}
with a generator
T\in\pi_{1}(S^{1})
acting by the automorphism
T
of
P^{\rm gp}=\bigoplus_{n\in{\mathds{Z}}}{\mathds{Z}}T^{n}={\mathds{Z}}[T,T^{-1}]
according to the glueing instruction for
{\mathcal{F}}
. This endows the stalk
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bar{x}}
with the structure of a free module of rank one under the group algebra
{\mathds{Z}}[\pi_{1}(S^{1})]={\mathds{Z}}[T,T^{-1}]
.

Proposition 3.4.9
.

There does not exist an (not necessarily charted!) inverse system
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
with affine transition maps of fs log schemes with inverse limit
X
.

Proof.

Suppose such a system exists, and let
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}
be the pull-back log structure along the projection
X\to X_{i}
, which is again fs. Then
{\mathcal{M}}_{X}=\varinjlim{\mathcal{M}}_{i}
and so
X=\varinjlim(X,{\mathcal{M}}_{i})
. We may therefore assume that the underlying scheme of
X_{i}
is
X
. Let
{\mathcal{F}}=\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm gp}_{X}
, which is a (Zariski) locally constant sheaf with fibre
{\mathds{Z}}[T,T^{-1}]
, and let
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}=\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm gp}_{i}
. Then
{\mathcal{F}}=\varinjlim{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
. We will make use of the following facts:

Lemma 3.4.10
.

Let
X
be a noetherian scheme. We work with étale sheaves of
{\mathds{Z}}
-modules on
X
.

(1)

If
{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
is a surjection and
{\mathcal{F}}
is constructible, then so is
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
.

(2)

A constructible sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
on
X
is locally constant if and only if for every specialization between geometric points
\overline{x}\leadsto\overline{y}
of
X
, the cospecialization map
{\mathcal{F}}_{\overline{y}}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{\overline{x}}
is an isomorphism. Every locally constant sheaf (not necessarily constructible) has this property.

(3)

If
{\mathcal{M}}
is an fs log structure on
X
, then the sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}=\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}^{\rm gp}
is constructible.

(4)

Moreover, for
{\mathcal{F}}
as in
3
, for every specialization
\overline{x}\leadsto\overline{y}
between geometric points of
X
, the cospecialization map
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bar{y}}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{\bar{x}}
is surjective.

Proof.

Assertion
1
is
[
SP
,
Proposition 09BH
]
.
2
is
[
SGA 43
, Exp. IX, Proposition 2.11]
,
and

3
and
4
follow from
[
Ogu18
, Theorem II 2.5.4]
.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i}
be the image of
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}\to{\mathcal{F}}
. Then
\varinjlim{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i}\to{\mathcal{F}}
is an isomorphism (being both injective and surjective). We claim that each
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i}
is a locally constant constructible sheaf of
{\mathds{Z}}
-modules. Indeed, it is constructible by
Lemma

lem:constr-sheaves
3
and
1
. Moreover, for every specialization
\overline{x}\leadsto\overline{y}
we have a commutative diagram of cospecialization maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathcal{F}}_{i})_{\bar{y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i})_{\bar{y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}_{\bar{y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathcal{F}}_{i})_{\bar{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i})_{\bar{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}_{\bar{x}}}

\scriptstyle\sim

Here, the left arrow is surjective by
Lemma

lem:constr-sheaves
4
and the right map is an isomorphism by
Lemma

lem:constr-sheaves
2
. It follows that the middle vertical arrow is an isomorphism, so
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i}
is locally constant by
Lemma

lem:constr-sheaves
2
.

We claim that there does not exist an inverse system of étale local systems of finite free
{\mathds{Z}}
-modules

\{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i}\}
on
X
with
{\mathcal{F}}=\varinjlim{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i}
, obtaining a contradiction. As in the construction above, we may assume that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i}
is a subsheaf of
{\mathcal{F}}
. Therefore the monodromy representation of
\pi_{1}^{\rm SGA3}(X,\overline{x})
on the fibre
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i,\bar{x}}
factors through
\pi_{1}(S^{1})
and thus yields a
{\mathds{Z}}[T,T^{-1}]
-submodule of
{\mathcal{F}}_{\bar{x}}\simeq{\mathds{Z}}[T,T^{-1}]
. As
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}_{i,\bar{x}}
is of finite rank as a
{\mathds{Z}}
-module, this can only be
0
, a contradiction.
∎

On the positive side, let us call a saturated log scheme
approximable
if it can be written as the limit of an affine charted inverse system of fs log schemes of finite type over
{\mathds{Z}}
. Our
Theorem

3.4.5
implies that if
X
is approximable and
Y\to X
is sfp, then
Y
is approximable as well.

3.5.
Smooth, étale, and Kummer étale maps of log schemes

We shall now extend the familiar notions of smooth, étale, and Kummer étale maps to arbitrary saturated log schemes.

Definition 3.5.1

(
[
Ill02
, 1.5, 1.6]
in the fs case)
.

A morphism of saturated log schemes
Y\to X
is
smooth
(resp.
étale
, resp.
Kummer étale
) if étale locally on source and target it admits a chart by a smooth (resp. étale, resp. Kummer étale) morphism of monoids
P\to Q
(with
\Sigma
the set of primes non-invertible on
X
) such that the induced strict map

Y\longrightarrow X\times_{{\mathds{A}}_{P}}{\mathds{A}}_{Q}

is smooth (resp. étale, resp. étale). In particular, such a morphism is locally sfp.

Remark 3.5.2
.

The above definitions are very similar to Kato’s criteria for smooth and étale morphisms
[
Kat89
, Theorem 3.5]
.
Proposition

3.5.3
below justifies that these notions are natural. We have refrained from discussing log differentials in this paper. However, we expect that smooth and étale morphisms can be characterized (among locally sfp morphisms) by a suitable infinitesimal lifting property.

Proposition 3.5.3
.

Let
f\colon Y\to X
be a morphism between saturated log schemes. The following conditions are equivalent.

(1)

The morphism
f
is smooth (resp. étale, resp. Kummer étale).

(2)

Locally on
X
and
Y
there exists a smooth (resp. étale, resp. Kummer étale) morphism of fs log schemes
Y_{0}\to X_{0}
and a map
X\to X_{0}
such that
Y
is isomorphic to the saturated pullback of
Y_{0}\to X_{0}
along
X\to X_{0}
.

Proof.

This is an analogue of
Proposition

3.3.3
for smooth (resp. étale, resp. Kummer étale) morphisms. The proof follows along the same lines. Suppose that
Y\to X
is smooth (resp. étale, resp. Kummer étale).
Étale locally
Y\to X
has a chart with monoid morphism
P\to Q
which by
Proposition

2.4.7
is the saturated pushout of a smooth (resp. étale, resp. Kummer étale) morphism
P_{0}\to Q_{0}
between fs monoids.
We put
P_{0}
as an initial monoid of a filtered colimit
P=\varinjlim_{i}P_{i}
with fs monoids
P_{i}
and with corresponding saturated pushout
